# DEMERGER SCHEMES - INDIAN CORPORATE LAW

## OVERVIEW

A demerger (or de-merger, spin-off) is a corporate restructuring where a company splits into two or more entities by transferring one or more undertakings to a separate company. The demerged company (transferor) transfers a business division/undertaking to the resulting company (transferee/new company), and shareholders of the demerged company receive shares in the resulting company in proportion to their existing shareholding (mirror principle).

**DEMERGER vs MERGER**:

| Parameter | Demerger | Merger |
|---|---|---|
| **Direction** | Company splits (1 → 2+) | Companies combine (2+ → 1) |
| **Purpose** | Unlock value, focus on core business | Synergies, economies of scale |
| **Transferor** | Continues to exist (retains remaining business) | Ceases to exist (dissolved) |
| **Consideration** | Shares issued by resulting company to demerged company shareholders | Shares issued by transferee to transferor shareholders |
| **Example** | Reliance Industries demerges Financial Services → Reliance Capital | HDFC Ltd merges into HDFC Bank |

**LEGAL DEFINITION (Section 2(19AA))**:
```
"Demerger" means transfer of one or more undertakings by demerged company to resulting company such that:
(a) ALL assets and liabilities of undertaking transferred
(b) Resulting company issues shares to shareholders of demerged company (on proportionate basis)
(c) Shareholders holding ≥75% shares in demerged undertaking become shareholders in resulting company
(d) Transfer is on "going concern" basis (business continues uninterrupted)
(e) Demerger is in accordance with Section 230-232 (scheme of arrangement)
```

**LEGAL FRAMEWORK**:
- **Companies Act 2013**: Sections 230-232 (Scheme of Arrangement), Section 2(19AA) (Definition)
- **Income Tax Act 1961**: Section 2(19AA) (Tax-neutral demerger conditions)
- **SEBI (LODR) Regulations 2015**: Listed company demerger disclosures, mirror principle
- **Competition Act 2002**: CCI approval (if thresholds crossed)

---

## 1. TYPES OF DEMERGERS

### 1.1 Full Demerger vs Partial Demerger

**Full Demerger (Complete Spin-Off)**:
```
Structure: Demerged company transfers entire business to resulting company, then liquidates
Result: Demerged company ceases to exist (like merger, but split not combine)
Rare in India (most demergers are partial)
```

**Partial Demerger (Standard)**:
```
Structure: Demerged company transfers one/some undertakings, retains core business
Result: Both demerged and resulting companies continue (2 listed companies post-demerger if demerged listed)
Example: Reliance Industries demerged Financial Services (resulting = Reliance Capital), retained Petrochemicals/Refining
```

### 1.2 Vertical vs Horizontal Demerger

**Vertical Demerger**:
```
Separate different stages of value chain
Example: Manufacturing + Retail → Manufacturing Co + Retail Co (separate upstream and downstream)
```

**Horizontal Demerger**:
```
Separate similar businesses/product lines
Example: Pharma (APIs + Formulations) → API Co + Formulations Co (separate product lines)
```

### 1.3 Equity Carve-Out vs Pure Spin-Off

**Equity Carve-Out (IPO of Subsidiary)**:
```
Structure: Resulting company lists via IPO, public owns minority, demerged company retains majority
Example: Reliance Retail IPO (Reliance Industries retains 70%, public 30%)
Not technically demerger (Reliance remains shareholder, shareholders don't receive shares directly)
```

**Pure Spin-Off (Demerger)**:
```
Structure: Resulting company shares distributed to demerged company shareholders (mirror principle)
Example: ITC demerges Hotels business → ITC shareholders receive ITC Hotels shares pro-rata
ITC shareholding: 0% in ITC Hotels (shareholders own both ITC + ITC Hotels separately)
```

**Example - Partial Demerger Structure**:
```
Before Demerger:
Company A (Conglomerate):
- Business 1: FMCG (₹500 cr revenue, ₹100 cr EBITDA)
- Business 2: Hotels (₹300 cr revenue, ₹50 cr EBITDA)
- Business 3: IT Services (₹200 cr revenue, ₹40 cr EBITDA)

Demerger:
- Hotels + IT Services transferred to Resulting Co (New Co)
- FMCG retained by Company A (Demerged Co)

After Demerger:
Company A (Demerged): FMCG only (₹500 cr revenue, ₹100 cr EBITDA)
New Co (Resulting): Hotels + IT Services (₹500 cr revenue, ₹90 cr EBITDA)

Shareholders: Own both A + New Co shares (proportionately)
```

---

## 2. NCLT DEMERGER PROCESS (Sections 230-232)

### 2.1 Pre-Scheme Preparation

**Step 1: Identify Undertaking**:
```
"Undertaking" = Business division capable of operating independently (Section 2(19AA))
Must have:
- Identifiable assets (plant, machinery, inventory, receivables)
- Identifiable liabilities (loans, payables specific to undertaking)
- Separate operations (distinct products/services/customers)
- Going concern (viable standalone business)

Example:
Company A: FMCG + Hotels
Undertaking (Hotels): Separate hotels, staff, brand, customers, suppliers, loans (identified) → Valid undertaking ✓

Not Undertaking: Corporate treasury function (not independent business, supports other divisions) → Invalid ✗
```

**Step 2: Valuation**:
```
Registered valuer prepares valuation of:
(a) Demerged Company (pre-demerger consolidated value)
(b) Demerged Undertaking (value of business being transferred)
(c) Resulting Company (value of undertaking on standalone basis)

Methods: DCF, NAV, Market Multiples
```

**Step 3: Share Allocation Ratio**:
```
Mirror Principle (SEBI): Resulting company shares issued to demerged company shareholders in SAME PROPORTION as demerged company shareholding

Formula: Shareholding % in Resulting Company = Shareholding % in Demerged Company (exactly mirrors)

Example:
Shareholder A: Owns 25% of Demerged Company
Post-Demerger: A owns 25% of Resulting Company (mirror shareholding)
```

**Step 4: Scheme Drafting**:
```
Demerger Scheme includes:
- Undertaking description (assets, liabilities, employees, contracts)
- Appointed Date (date from which undertaking deemed transferred, retrospective accounting)
- Effective Date (date NCLT order becomes operative)
- Share allocation (resulting company shares to demerged shareholders, exchange ratio)
- Employees (transfer on existing terms, continuity of service)
- Contracts (assignment to resulting company, consent from counterparties if required)
- Liabilities (specified liabilities transferred, demerged company indemnifies if any missed)
```

### 2.2 NCLT Process (Same as Merger, 270-365 days)

**Process identical to merger** (see IL-CORP-MERGER-PROCESS-NCLT):
1. Board approval (demerged + resulting companies)
2. NCLT application (Form NCLT-1)
3. NCLT directions (convene shareholder/creditor meetings)
4. Meetings (75% in value + 50% in number approval)
5. Regulatory approvals (ROC, IT, SEBI, Stock Exchanges, CCI)
6. Final NCLT hearing (scheme sanction)
7. ROC filing (Form INC-28)
8. Implementation (shares issued, assets/liabilities transferred)

**Key Difference**: Resulting company may be newly incorporated (vs merger where transferee already exists).

**Example - Timeline**:
```
Day 0: Board approves demerger scheme
Day 30: Incorporate Resulting Co (new company, if not existing)
Day 60: NCLT application filed (demerged company jurisdiction)
Day 90: NCLT first hearing (directions for meetings)
Day 150: Shareholder/creditor meetings (75% approval achieved)
Day 240: Regulatory approvals obtained (ROC, IT, SEBI, Stock Exchange)
Day 300: NCLT final hearing (scheme sanctioned, effective date specified)
Day 330: Implementation (resulting company shares issued to demerged shareholders, assets/liabilities transferred)
```

---

## 3. MIRROR PRINCIPLE (SEBI)

### 3.1 Proportionate Share Allocation

**SEBI Requirement (Sterlite Industries case)**: Resulting company shares must be issued to demerged company shareholders in EXACT SAME PROPORTION as their shareholding in demerged company.

**Rationale**: Prevent wealth transfer between shareholders (if disproportionate, some shareholders gain, others lose).

**Formula**:
```
Shareholding % in Resulting Co = Shareholding % in Demerged Co

OR

Shares received in Resulting Co ÷ Total Resulting Co shares = Shares held in Demerged Co ÷ Total Demerged Co shares
```

**Example - Mirror Principle Application**:
```
Demerged Company A (10 crore shares):
- Promoter: 6 crore shares (60%)
- PE Investor: 2 crore shares (20%)
- Public: 2 crore shares (20%)

Demerger: Hotels division transferred to Resulting Company B
Resulting Company B issues: 5 crore shares

Mirror Allocation:
- Promoter: 60% × 5 crore = 3 crore B shares (owns 60% of B)
- PE Investor: 20% × 5 crore = 1 crore B shares (owns 20% of B)
- Public: 20% × 5 crore = 1 crore B shares (owns 20% of B)

Check: Each shareholder's % ownership in B = % ownership in A (mirror maintained ✓)

Post-Demerger Holdings:
- Promoter: 6 crore A shares (60% of A) + 3 crore B shares (60% of B)
- PE Investor: 2 crore A shares (20% of A) + 1 crore B shares (20% of B)
- Public: 2 crore A shares (20% of A) + 1 crore B shares (20% of B)
```

### 3.2 Violations and Consequences

**Non-Proportionate Allocation = Violation**:
```
Example (Violation):
Demerger Company: Promoter 60%, Public 40%
Resulting Company (incorrect): Promoter 70%, Public 30%

Issue: Promoter receives disproportionately more shares (wealth transfer from public to promoter)

SEBI Action:
- Reject scheme (scheme approval withheld)
- Require rectification (adjust allocation to 60:40 mirror)
```

**Exceptions (Rare)**:
- ESOP shares in resulting company (not mirror, allocated to employees)
- Preferential issue in resulting company (new investor subscribes, dilutes mirror slightly - requires separate shareholder approval)

---

## 4. VALUATION AND SHARE EXCHANGE RATIO

### 4.1 Valuation Methodology

**Demerged Company Valuation (Pre-Demerger)**:
```
Sum-of-Parts Valuation:
Business 1 (retained): ₹1,000 crore (DCF valuation)
Business 2 (demerged): ₹500 crore (DCF valuation)
Corporate costs: (₹50 crore) (allocated to retained business)
Total: ₹1,450 crore

Per Share Value: ₹1,450 cr ÷ 10 cr shares = ₹145/share
```

**Post-Demerger Valuation**:
```
Demerged Company (retained business): ₹950 crore (₹1,000 cr - ₹50 cr corporate costs)
Resulting Company (demerged business): ₹500 crore

Check: ₹950 cr + ₹500 cr = ₹1,450 crore (pre-demerger value preserved ✓)
```

**Share Allocation**:
```
Resulting Company: Issues 2 crore shares (decided by scheme)
Per Share Value: ₹500 cr ÷ 2 cr = ₹250/share

Shareholder A (owns 1 crore shares in Demerged Company = 10%):
Receives: 10% × 2 cr = 20 lakh Resulting Company shares

Shareholder A Pre-Demerger Wealth:
1 cr shares × ₹145/share = ₹145 crore

Shareholder A Post-Demerger Wealth:
Demerged Co: 1 cr shares × ₹95/share = ₹95 crore (₹950 cr ÷ 10 cr shares)
Resulting Co: 20 lakh shares × ₹250/share = ₹50 crore
Total: ₹95 cr + ₹50 cr = ₹145 crore (wealth preserved ✓)
```

### 4.2 Dissenting Shareholders

**Same as Merger**: Shareholders who vote against scheme can object at NCLT final hearing.

**Common Objections**:
- Undervaluation of demerged undertaking (shareholders lose value)
- Inappropriate allocation of corporate costs/debts (demerged undertaking burdened with excessive liabilities)
- Non-mirror allocation (some shareholders receive disproportionate shares)

**NCLT Powers**: Modify scheme (adjust valuation, share allocation, cost/debt allocation) or reject scheme.

---

## 5. TAX-NEUTRAL DEMERGER (Section 2(19AA), Income Tax Act)

### 5.1 Conditions for Tax Neutrality

**Section 2(19AA) Definition** (Tax-neutral demerger conditions):
```
1. Transfer of undertaking on "going concern" basis (business continues uninterrupted)
2. ALL assets and liabilities of undertaking transferred (cannot cherry-pick)
3. Resulting company issues shares worth ≥75% of net worth of demerged undertaking
4. Shareholders holding ≥75% of demerged company shares become shareholders of resulting company
5. No consideration other than shares (no cash payment to demerged company or its shareholders)
6. Demerger pursuant to court-approved scheme (NCLT/NCLT approval)
```

**If Conditions Met**:
- **Demerged Company**: No capital gains tax on undertaking transfer (Section 47(vib))
- **Resulting Company**: Inherits tax attributes of demerged undertaking (carry-forward losses, MAT credit - proportionate) (Section 72A(5))
- **Shareholders**: No capital gains tax on share receipt (Section 47(vii))
  - Cost of acquisition split: Original cost allocated between demerged shares (retained) and resulting shares (received) based on Net Asset Value ratio

**Example - Tax-Neutral Demerger**:
```
Demerged Company A:
- Undertaking (Hotels): Assets ₹800 cr (book value), ₹1,200 cr (fair value)
- Capital gain if sold: ₹400 cr (taxable @ 25% = ₹100 cr tax)

Tax-Neutral Demerger:
- Hotels transferred to Resulting Company B (Section 2(19AA) conditions satisfied)
- Section 47(vib): Transfer exempt from capital gains tax (₹0 tax vs ₹100 cr if sold)
- Section 72A(5): B inherits A's carry-forward losses (proportionate, e.g., ₹200 cr losses allocated to hotels undertaking)

Shareholder X:
- Original Cost: 1 lakh A shares @ ₹50/share = ₹50 lakh
- Receives: 10,000 B shares (mirror allocation)

Cost Allocation (NAV method):
- A NAV post-demerger: ₹80/share (retained business)
- B NAV: ₹120/share (demerged undertaking)
- Ratio: 80:120 = 40:60

Cost Allocation:
- A shares (retained): ₹50 lakh × 40% = ₹20 lakh (₹20 cost per A share)
- B shares (received): ₹50 lakh × 60% = ₹30 lakh (₹300 cost per B share)

Future Sale of B shares @ ₹500/share:
Capital Gain: (₹500 - ₹300) × 10,000 = ₹20 lakh (taxable when sold, not at demerger)
```

### 5.2 Non-Tax-Neutral Demerger

**If Section 2(19AA) conditions NOT met** (e.g., cash consideration paid, <75% shareholding continuity):
- Demerged company: Capital gains tax on undertaking transfer (fair value - book value taxable)
- Shareholders: Tax on share receipt (value of shares received taxable as dividend)

**Example - Non-Tax-Neutral**:
```
Demerger with Cash Component:
- Hotels undertaking transferred to Resulting Co (₹500 cr fair value)
- Resulting Co issues: ₹300 cr shares + ₹200 cr cash to Demerged Co

Issue: Cash consideration paid → Violates Section 2(19AA) condition (no consideration other than shares)

Tax Consequences:
- Demerged Co: Capital gains tax on ₹500 cr (fair value) - ₹300 cr (book value) = ₹200 cr gain (₹50 cr tax @ 25%)
- Shareholders: ₹200 cr cash distributed treated as dividend (taxed in hands of Demerged Co @ 30%+ as dividend distribution tax)
```

---

## 6. ACCOUNTING TREATMENT (Ind AS)

### 6.1 Demerged Company Accounting

**At Appointed Date** (retrospective accounting):
```
Journal Entry (Demerged Company A):
Dr. Resulting Company B (Investment) ₹500 cr (value of undertaking transferred)
Cr. Net Assets (Undertaking) ₹500 cr (eliminate assets/liabilities transferred)

Post-Demerger:
- A retains investment in B: ₹500 cr (shown as investment if A holds B shares)
- OR
- A distributes B shares to shareholders: Eliminate investment, reduce reserves ₹500 cr (if mirror distribution)
```

**Example**:
```
Demerged Company A (Pre-Demerger Balance Sheet):
Assets: ₹1,500 cr (FMCG ₹1,000 cr + Hotels ₹500 cr)
Liabilities: ₹600 cr (FMCG ₹400 cr + Hotels ₹200 cr)
Net Assets: ₹900 cr
Equity: ₹900 cr (10 cr shares @ ₹90/share)

Demerger (Hotels transferred to Resulting Company B):
Assets transferred: ₹500 cr
Liabilities transferred: ₹200 cr
Net Assets transferred: ₹300 cr

Journal Entry:
Dr. Investment in B ₹300 cr
Dr. Liabilities (Hotels) ₹200 cr
Cr. Assets (Hotels) ₹500 cr

A's Balance Sheet Post-Demerger:
Assets: ₹1,000 cr (FMCG retained) + ₹300 cr (investment in B) = ₹1,300 cr
Liabilities: ₹400 cr (FMCG)
Net Assets: ₹900 cr (unchanged)
Equity: ₹900 cr

OR (if B shares distributed to shareholders immediately):
Dr. Reserves ₹300 cr
Cr. Investment in B ₹300 cr
(A's equity reduces to ₹600 cr, shareholders now own A + B separately)
```

### 6.2 Resulting Company Accounting

**At Incorporation**:
```
Resulting Company B receives:
- Assets: ₹500 cr (at book value from A)
- Liabilities: ₹200 cr (from A)
- Net Assets: ₹300 cr

B issues shares: 2 cr shares (₹10 face value = ₹20 cr, balance ₹280 cr to reserves)

B's Balance Sheet (Day 1):
Assets: ₹500 cr
Liabilities: ₹200 cr
Share Capital: ₹20 cr (2 cr shares @ ₹10 face value)
Reserves: ₹280 cr
Total Equity: ₹300 cr
```

---

## 7. REGULATORY APPROVALS

### 7.1 SEBI and Stock Exchange Approvals (Listed Companies)

**SEBI Observation Letter**:
- SEBI reviews: Mirror principle compliance, valuation fairness, disclosure adequacy
- SEBI may require: Independent valuation, fairness opinion by merchant banker, enhanced disclosures

**Stock Exchange In-Principle Approval**:
- Resulting company listing: If demerged company listed, resulting company also lists (automatic listing)
- Listing requirements: Minimum public shareholding (25%), market capitalization (₹500 cr for main board)

**Example**:
```
Demerged Company: Listed on NSE (market cap ₹5,000 cr, promoter 55%, public 45%)
Resulting Company: Hotels division (estimated market cap ₹2,000 cr)

Listing Compliance:
- Public shareholding: 45% (mirrors demerged company, ≥25% required ✓)
- Market cap: ₹2,000 cr (≥₹500 cr main board requirement ✓)
- Result: Resulting company eligible for NSE main board listing ✓

Stock Exchange In-Principle Approval:
- NSE grants approval (subject to scheme sanction by NCLT)
- Trading begins: Post-NCLT order + ROC filing (typically 30 days after NCLT)
```

### 7.2 CCI Approval (Competition Commission)

**Same thresholds as merger** (see IL-CORP-MERGER-PROCESS-NCLT):
- Combined assets >₹2,000 cr OR Combined turnover >₹6,000 cr
- OR Target assets >₹750 cr OR Target turnover >₹2,250 cr

**Demerger Specific**: Usually NO CCI approval required (demerger splits business, reduces market concentration, unlikely to create competition concerns).

**Exception**: If demerger creates dominant player (e.g., all hotel assets consolidated into one resulting company while competitors remain fragmented).

---

## 8. POST-DEMERGER INTEGRATION

### 8.1 Operational Separation

**Day 1-30 (Transition Services Agreement)**:
```
Issue: Resulting company may depend on demerged company for shared services (IT, HR, Finance, Legal)
Solution: Transition Services Agreement (TSA) - Demerged company provides services for 6-12 months (fee-based)

Example:
Hotels demerged from FMCG conglomerate
TSA: FMCG provides IT support, payroll processing, legal services to Hotels for 12 months (₹2 cr/month fee)
Timeline: Hotels builds standalone IT/HR/Legal within 12 months, then TSA terminates
```

**Day 1-100 (Operational Independence)**:
- **Employees**: Transfer employment contracts (hotels staff to Resulting Co), continuity of service
- **Contracts**: Assign customer/supplier contracts (consent required if contracts have non-assignment clauses)
- **Licenses**: Transfer regulatory licenses (hotel licenses, trade licenses, GST registration)
- **IP**: Assign trademarks, brands (hotels brand to Resulting Co)
- **Real Estate**: Transfer property titles (hotel properties to Resulting Co)
- **IT Systems**: Separate ERP, email, intranet (build standalone or subscribe to new systems)

### 8.2 Listing and Trading

**Resulting Company Listing** (if demerged listed):
```
Timeline:
- NCLT order: Day 300
- ROC filing: Day 310
- Shares credited to shareholders: Day 320 (demat account, ISIN allotted)
- Trading begins: Day 330 (NSE/BSE trading commences)

Shareholder Impact:
- Pre-demerger: Owns 1 lakh A shares (worth ₹145 lakh at ₹145/share)
- Post-demerger: Owns 1 lakh A shares (₹95/share) + 10,000 B shares (₹250/share)
- Total value: ₹95 lakh + ₹25 lakh = ₹120 lakh (Day 1 trading may be volatile, ₹145 lakh pre-demerger value not guaranteed)

Lock-in: No lock-in for public shareholders (freely tradable from Day 1)
Promoter lock-in: If promoter holding increases post-demerger (due to rounding), excess locked in for 1 year
```

---

## 9. STRATEGIC RATIONALE FOR DEMERGERS

### 9.1 Unlock Conglomerate Discount

**Conglomerate Discount**: Diversified companies trade at discount vs sum-of-parts (market difficulty valuing unrelated businesses).

**Example**:
```
Conglomerate A (FMCG + Hotels):
- FMCG value (standalone): ₹1,000 cr (10x EBITDA)
- Hotels value (standalone): ₹500 cr (10x EBITDA)
- Sum-of-parts: ₹1,500 cr
- Actual market cap (conglomerate): ₹1,200 cr (20% discount)

Demerger:
- FMCG Co: ₹1,000 cr (post-demerger market cap, pure-play FMCG valued at 10x)
- Hotels Co: ₹500 cr (post-demerger market cap, pure-play hotels valued at 10x)
- Total value: ₹1,500 cr (conglomerate discount eliminated, 25% value creation)

Shareholder Benefit: ₹1,500 cr ÷ 10 cr shares = ₹150/share (vs ₹120/share pre-demerger, 25% gain)
```

### 9.2 Management Focus

**Issue**: Conglomerate management distracted by unrelated businesses (FMCG CEO managing hotels, suboptimal).

**Solution**: Demerger creates focused management teams (FMCG CEO focuses on FMCG, Hotels CEO focuses on hotels).

**Result**: Better operational performance (focused strategy, specialized expertise, faster decision-making).

### 9.3 Fundraising and M&A

**Separate Capital Allocation**:
- Hotels (capital-intensive): Raises debt for hotel acquisitions (optimal leverage for hotels)
- FMCG (working capital-intensive): Raises equity for brand expansion (optimal leverage for FMCG)
- Pre-demerger: Single balance sheet, suboptimal leverage for both (FMCG wants less debt, hotels want more debt)

**M&A Flexibility**:
- Resulting company can be sold separately (strategic buyer interested in hotels, not FMCG)
- Pre-demerger: Must sell entire conglomerate (fewer buyers, lower valuation)

---

## 10. BEST PRACTICES

### 10.1 Undertaking Definition

**Clear Boundaries**: Define undertaking with precision (assets, liabilities, employees, contracts listed in schedule).

**Avoid Disputes**:
- Shared assets: Allocate clearly (office building shared → allocate floors, or sell and distribute proceeds)
- Shared contracts: Assign or duplicate (group insurance policy → separate policies for each entity)
- Corporate costs: Allocate based on usage (IT costs → allocate based on employee count)

**Example - Shared Asset Allocation**:
```
Head Office Building: 10 floors (₹100 cr value)
- FMCG uses 7 floors
- Hotels uses 3 floors

Option 1 (Physical Division):
- FMCG retains 7 floors (₹70 cr value)
- Hotels gets 3 floors (₹30 cr value transferred to Resulting Co)

Option 2 (Sell and Distribute):
- Sell building for ₹100 cr
- FMCG gets ₹70 cr (buys new office)
- Hotels gets ₹30 cr (buys new office)
- Clean separation, no shared asset post-demerger
```

### 10.2 Employee Communication

**Uncertainty**: Employees uncertain about employment continuity, compensation, career prospects post-demerger.

**Communication Plan**:
- Announce demerger rationale (strategic focus, unlock value, growth prospects)
- Employee town halls (Q&A with leadership, address concerns)
- One-on-one meetings (affected employees, discuss transfer, role, compensation)
- Retention bonuses (key employees, prevent attrition during uncertainty)

**Continuity of Service**: Section 230-232 schemes preserve employee rights (service continuity, PF, gratuity).

---

## STRATEGIC CONSIDERATIONS

### When to Choose Demerger vs Asset Sale (Slump Sale)

**Choose Demerger When**:
- Tax-neutral treatment required (preserve tax attributes, avoid capital gains tax)
- Shareholders want to own both businesses (participate in upside of both entities)
- Resulting company needs listing (automatic listing if demerged company listed)
- Long-term strategic separation (both businesses continue independently)

**Choose Asset Sale (Slump Sale) When**:
- Speed critical (2-4 months vs 9-12 months for demerger)
- Buyer wants 100% ownership (demerger = distributed to shareholders, buyer must make separate offer)
- Tax-neutral not required (can absorb capital gains tax)
- Demerged company wants cash (asset sale = cash proceeds, demerger = no cash to company)

---

## COMPLIANCE CHECKLIST

**Pre-Application**:
- [ ] Board approval (demerged company, resulting company if existing)
- [ ] Incorporate resulting company (if new entity, capital ₹5 lakh typical)
- [ ] Due diligence (undertaking identification, asset/liability listing)
- [ ] Valuation (registered valuer, demerged company + undertaking + resulting company)
- [ ] Draft scheme (comprehensive, mirror principle, appointed date, effective date)

**NCLT Application**:
- [ ] File Form NCLT-1 (demerged company jurisdiction)
- [ ] Attach scheme, board resolutions, valuation, financials (3 years), shareholder/creditor lists
- [ ] Pay NCLT fees (₹5-10 lakh)
- [ ] First hearing (NCLT directions for meetings)

**Meetings**:
- [ ] Convene shareholder meeting (demerged company, 21-day notice, 75% in value + 50% in number)
- [ ] Convene creditor meeting (demerged company, same threshold)
- [ ] File meeting results (Form CA-4 within 7 days)

**Regulatory Approvals**:
- [ ] ROC no-objection (demerged + resulting companies)
- [ ] Income Tax clearance (Section 2(19AA) tax-neutral conditions verified)
- [ ] SEBI observation letter (if listed, mirror principle compliance)
- [ ] Stock exchange in-principle approval (resulting company listing)
- [ ] CCI approval (if thresholds crossed, rare for demergers)

**Final NCLT Hearing**:
- [ ] File regulatory approvals with NCLT
- [ ] Respond to objections (dissenting shareholders)
- [ ] NCLT passes order (scheme sanctioned, effective date)

**Implementation**:
- [ ] ROC filing (Form INC-28, both companies)
- [ ] Issue shares (resulting company shares to demerged shareholders, mirror allocation)
- [ ] Transfer assets/liabilities (deed of conveyance, assignment)
- [ ] Transfer employees (employment contracts, PF transfer, service continuity)
- [ ] Update licenses, contracts (resulting company replaces demerged company)
- [ ] List resulting company (if demerged listed, trading begins)

---

## LEGAL DISCLAIMER

This protocol provides information on demerger schemes under Indian law. It is for informational purposes only and does not constitute legal, financial, tax, or investment advice. Companies should consult qualified corporate lawyers, chartered accountants, company secretaries, registered valuers, and merchant bankers for demerger transactions. NCLT procedures, tax treatment, SEBI regulations, and listing requirements are subject to change. Demerger timelines and costs vary based on transaction complexity.

---

**Last Updated**: December 5, 2025
**Version**: 1.0.0
**Protocol ID**: IL-CORP-DEMERGER-SCHEMES

## IL-CORP-DIRECTOR-DUTIES.md
---

# IL-CORP-DIRECTOR-DUTIES
## Fiduciary Duties, Duty of Care, and Director Liability

---
id: IL-CORP-DIRECTOR-DUTIES
version: 1.0.0
category: corporate_law
subcategory: board_management

  - Fiduciary Duties Overview
  - Duty to Act in Good Faith
  - Duty to Exercise Independent Judgment
  - Duty of Care, Skill, and Diligence
  - Duty to Avoid Conflicts of Interest
  - Duty Not to Achieve Undue Gains
  - Business Judgment Rule
  - Director Liability and Penalties
  - Lifting of Corporate Veil
  - D&O Insurance
dependencies:
  - IL-CORP-DIRECTORS-APPOINTMENT (Director appointment and removal)
  - IL-CORP-BOARD-MEETINGS (Board decision-making)
  - IL-COMPANIES-ACT (Companies Act 2013, Section 166)
related_protocols:
  foundation:
    - IL-CORP-RELATED-PARTY-TRANSACTIONS (Related party dealings)
    - IL-CORP-SHAREHOLDERS-RIGHTS (Director-shareholder relationship)
  remedies:
    - CIVIC-WRIT-BASICS (Writ against directors for breach)
    - CIVIC-TRIBUNALS (NCLT for director liability)
landmark_cases:
  - Re City Equitable Fire Insurance [1925] Ch 407 (Duty of care standard)
  - Salomon v. Salomon & Co [1897] AC 22 (Corporate veil, limited liability)
  - Regal (Hastings) Ltd v. Gulliver [1942] 1 All ER 378 (No-profit rule)
  - Smith v. Van Gorkom 488 A.2d 858 (Delaware, 1985) (Business judgment rule)
  - Howard Smith Ltd v. Ampol Petroleum Ltd [1974] AC 821 (Proper purpose doctrine)
---

## Overview

**Director's Duties** = Legal obligations directors owe to company (not shareholders) arising from their position as fiduciaries.

**Legal Framework**:
- **Section 166**: General duties of directors (codification of common law fiduciary duties)
- **Section 184**: Disclosure of interest in contracts/arrangements
- **Section 185-188**: Loans to directors, related party transactions
- **Section 166(5)**: Duty to attend board meetings
- **Section 339**: Liability for fraudulent conduct

**Key Principle**: Directors are **trustees** of company's assets, **agents** of company (not individual shareholders). They must act in **company's best interest**, not personal interest or individual shareholder's interest.

---

## Fiduciary Duties Overview (Section 166)

### Statutory Codification

**Section 166**: Every director shall:
1. Act in accordance with company's articles (Section 166(1))
2. Act in good faith to promote company's objects (Section 166(2))
3. Exercise duties with due and reasonable care, skill, and diligence (Section 166(3))
4. Not involve in situation creating conflict of interest (Section 166(4))
5. Not achieve undue gain or advantage (Section 166(5))
6. Not assign office (Section 166(6))

**Penalty for Breach** (Section 166(7)):
- Director liable to company for:
  - Restitution of profits made (disgorgement)
  - Compensation for losses caused to company
  - Criminal prosecution (if fraud involved)

---

## 1. Duty to Act in Accordance with AOA (Section 166(1))

### Scope

**Rule**: Directors must act within powers conferred by **Articles of Association**.

**Examples**:
✅ If AOA empowers board to borrow up to ₹50 lakh → Board can borrow ≤₹50 lakh (without shareholder approval)
❌ If board borrows ₹1 crore (beyond AOA limit) → **Ultra vires directors**, breach of duty

**Rationale**: AOA is company's constitution (approved by shareholders). Directors cannot exceed powers granted by shareholders via AOA.

---

### Proper Purpose Doctrine

**Rule**: Directors must exercise powers for **proper purpose** (purpose for which power conferred), not collateral/improper purpose.

**Landmark Case: Howard Smith Ltd v. Ampol Petroleum Ltd (1974)**

**Facts**:
- Company had 2 major shareholders: Ampol (55%) and Bulkships (45%)
- Board issued new shares to Howard Smith (diluting Ampol to 37%, Bulkships to 31%, Howard Smith 32%)
- Purpose claimed: Raise capital for company
- Actual purpose: Dilute Ampol's majority control (Ampol opposed takeover, board wanted Howard Smith as partner)

**Held**:
- Share issuance power must be exercised for **proper purpose** (raising capital)
- Using share issuance to alter control = **Improper purpose** (even if directors believed it was in company's interest)
- Share issuance set aside (invalid)

**Principle**: Directors cannot use share issuance power to manipulate control/voting, even if they believe it's in company's interest. Power must be used for purpose for which conferred (raising capital, not altering control).

**Application**: If directors issue shares to:
✅ Raise capital for expansion → Proper purpose (valid)
✅ Fund acquisition (genuine business need) → Proper purpose (valid)
❌ Dilute hostile takeover bidder → Improper purpose (invalid, defensive tactic)
❌ Reward friendly investors, punish opponents → Improper purpose (invalid)

---

## 2. Duty to Act in Good Faith (Section 166(2))

### Subjective vs Objective Test

**Rule**: Directors must act in **good faith** to promote company's objects, for benefit of members as a whole.

**Subjective Test**: What directors **honestly believed** was in company's interest
**Objective Test**: What **reasonable director** would believe was in company's interest

**Indian Law**: Predominantly **subjective** (if directors honestly believed act was in company's interest, good faith satisfied), BUT courts apply **objective reasonableness** check (honesty alone insufficient if decision patently unreasonable).

---

### Benefit of Company vs Individual Shareholders

**General Rule**: Directors owe duty to **company** (legal entity), not individual shareholders.

**Exception**: In specific situations, directors must consider **shareholders as a whole**:
- Winding up (company insolvent, shareholders' interests priority shifts to creditors)
- Takeover situations (shareholders' interests in receiving fair price)

**Landmark Case: Percival v. Wright (1902)**
**Facts**: Directors purchased shares from shareholders at ₹12.50 without disclosing company was in takeover talks (shares worth ₹30).
**Held**: Directors owed no duty to disclose to individual shareholders. Duty owed to company, not individual members.
**Principle**: No fiduciary relationship between directors and individual shareholders (unless special circumstances like takeover).

**Exception**: Modern law recognizes directors must consider **creditors' interests** when company nearing insolvency (wrongful trading provisions).

---

## 3. Duty to Exercise Independent Judgment (Section 166(3))

### No Fettering of Discretion

**Rule**: Directors must exercise **independent judgment**, not blindly follow instructions from shareholders, promoters, or external parties.

**Prohibited**:
❌ Director acts as "puppet" of controlling shareholder (rubber-stamps all decisions)
❌ Director signs blank board resolutions (allowing others to fill in later)
❌ Director abdicates decision-making to CEO/promoter (doesn't apply mind)

**Allowed**:
✅ Director seeks advice from experts (lawyers, CAs, consultants) before deciding
✅ Director considers shareholder views BUT makes independent assessment
✅ Nominee director represents nominating party's views BUT exercises independent judgment (owes duty to company)

**Nominee Directors**: Despite being nominated by specific shareholder/lender, nominee director owes fiduciary duty to **company** (not just nominating party). Must exercise independent judgment.

**Landmark Case: Scottish Co-operative Wholesale Society v. Meyer (1959)**
**Facts**: Nominee directors (appointed by parent company) acted solely in parent's interest, ignoring minority shareholders' rights.
**Held**: Nominee directors breached fiduciary duty. Despite being nominees, they owe duty to **company**, must exercise independent judgment.

---

## 4. Duty of Care, Skill, and Diligence (Section 166(3))

### Standard of Care

**Section 166(3)**: Director shall exercise duties with **due and reasonable care, skill, and diligence**, exercising **independent judgment**.

**Test** (Dual Standard):
1. **Subjective Test**: Care, skill expected from director based on their **actual knowledge, skill, experience**
2. **Objective Test**: Care, skill expected from **reasonably diligent person** in director's position

**Landmark Case: Re City Equitable Fire Insurance (1925)**

**Facts**: Company's fire insurance business collapsed (heavy losses). Directors claimed they relied on managing director, didn't scrutinize accounts.

**Held**: Directors' duty of care assessed on:
1. **Subjective Test**: Director need only exhibit care, skill they **actually possess** (not expected to have skills they don't have)
2. **Objective Test**: Director expected to exhibit care **reasonable person** in their position would show

**Three Principles**:
1. **Skill**: Director need not exhibit greater skill than they actually possess. Finance expert held to higher standard than non-expert director.
2. **Delegation**: Directors can delegate to officers/committees (need not personally perform every task). BUT must exercise general supervision.
3. **Attendance**: Directors not bound to attend every board meeting. BUT regular attendance expected (absenting for 12 months = vacation of office u/s 167).

**Modern Trend**: Indian law adopts **higher objective standard** - Directors expected to be **reasonably informed**, apply business judgment. Cannot claim ignorance of basic financial health, major transactions.

---

### Specific Care Duties

**a. Duty to Attend Board Meetings** (Section 166(5)):
- Directors must attend board meetings regularly
- Repeated absence (12 months) = Automatic vacation of office (Section 167(1)(b))

**b. Duty to Be Informed**:
- Read board papers, financial statements, proposals
- Ask questions, seek clarifications
- Cannot blindly sign resolutions without understanding

**c. Duty to Monitor**:
- Oversee management performance
- Review financial position regularly
- Ensure compliance with laws, regulations

**d. Duty to Act Timely**:
- Take decisions without undue delay
- Address red flags, warning signs promptly

---

### Business Judgment Rule

**Definition**: Courts defer to directors' **business decisions** (made in good faith, with reasonable information, without conflict of interest), even if decision ultimately fails.

**Rationale**: Directors are business experts, courts are not. Hindsight should not be used to judge business decisions. Protects directors from liability for honest errors of judgment.

**Requirements for Protection**:
1. ✅ **Good Faith**: Decision made honestly, believing it was in company's interest
2. ✅ **Informed Basis**: Directors reasonably informed (reviewed proposals, sought expert advice)
3. ✅ **No Conflict of Interest**: Decision not tainted by self-interest
4. ✅ **Rational Basis**: Decision has rational business justification (not arbitrary)

**Landmark Case: Smith v. Van Gorkom (Delaware, 1985)**

**Facts**:
- Board approved company sale at $55/share in 2-hour meeting (without reviewing valuation, expert fairness opinion, or negotiating price)
- Shareholders sued directors for breach of duty of care (inadequate deliberation)

**Held**:
- Directors breached duty of care (grossly negligent)
- Business judgment rule NOT applicable (directors not reasonably informed)
- Insufficient deliberation (2-hour meeting for $690 million sale), no valuation analysis, no expert opinion

**Principle**: Business judgment rule protects directors ONLY if decision made on **informed basis**. Directors must:
- Review materials (proposals, valuations, expert reports)
- Deliberate sufficiently (2-hour meeting inadequate for major transaction)
- Document decision-making process (board minutes, resolutions)

**Impact**: Post-Van Gorkom, boards conduct thorough deliberations for major transactions (fairness opinions, independent valuations, extended board meetings, detailed minutes).

---

### Indian Application of Business Judgment Rule

**Indian Courts**: Recognize business judgment rule **implicitly** (courts reluctant to second-guess commercial decisions).

**Example**: Court will not interfere if board decides to:
- Enter new market (even if venture fails)
- Acquire company (even if acquisition underperforms)
- Divest business unit (even if buyer profits later)

**Provided**: Decision made in good faith, with reasonable information, without conflict.

**Exception**: Courts intervene if:
- Decision made fraudulently, in bad faith
- Self-dealing (directors benefited personally)
- Gross negligence (no reasonable basis for decision)

---

## 5. Duty to Avoid Conflicts of Interest (Section 166(4))

### No-Conflict Rule

**Rule**: Director must not place themselves in position where personal interest **conflicts** with company's interest.

**Examples of Conflicts**:
❌ Director has personal interest in contract with company (owns supplier company, company buying from supplier)
❌ Director competes with company (starts rival business)
❌ Director takes corporate opportunity (company had opportunity, director diverts to self)

---

### Disclosure of Interest (Section 184)

**Mandatory Disclosure**:
- Director must disclose **nature of interest** in contracts/arrangements (at board meeting)
- Disclosure required BEFORE contract entered, OR at first board meeting after interest arises
- Form MBP-1: Register of interests maintained

**Example**:
- Director A owns 50% in Supplier Co.
- Company proposes to buy goods from Supplier Co.
- Director A must disclose: "I hold 50% shares in Supplier Co. I am interested in this transaction."
- Board (excluding interested director) votes on transaction

**Non-Interested Directors**: Only **non-interested directors** can approve related party transaction. Interested director cannot vote (Section 184(2)).

**Penalty for Non-Disclosure** (Section 184(3)):
- Fine: ₹1 lakh - ₹5 lakh
- Contract voidable at company's option

---

### Corporate Opportunity Doctrine

**Rule**: Director cannot usurp **corporate opportunity** (opportunity belonging to company) for personal benefit.

**Test for Corporate Opportunity**:
1. Was opportunity **in line of company's business**?
2. Did company have **financial ability** to pursue opportunity?
3. Did director learn of opportunity **through directorship** (using company's resources/information)?

**If YES to above** → Corporate opportunity. Director must:
- Offer opportunity to company first
- If company declines (after full disclosure), director may pursue personally

**Landmark Case: Regal (Hastings) Ltd v. Gulliver (1942)**

**Facts**:
- Regal Ltd wanted to acquire cinema lease (needed £5,000 capital)
- Regal couldn't raise capital, directors subscribed personally, acquired lease through separate company
- Later, Regal sold cinema business (including directors' company) at profit
- Directors made personal profit

**Held**:
- Directors liable to account for profit to Regal (even though Regal couldn't have pursued opportunity)
- No-profit rule: Directors cannot profit from position (even if company couldn't have profited)
- Directors must offer opportunity to company first, obtain informed board approval before pursuing personally

**Principle**: **No-profit rule** is strict. Directors cannot retain profit made using:
- Company's property, information, or opportunity
- Position as director
Even if company could not have exploited opportunity, director must account for profit.

**Exception**: Directors can pursue corporate opportunity if:
- Full disclosure to board (all material facts)
- Board (of non-interested directors) approves director pursuing opportunity
- Company genuinely unable/unwilling to pursue (documented in board minutes)

---

## 6. Duty Not to Achieve Undue Gain (Section 166(5))

### No-Profit Rule

**Section 166(5)**: Director shall not, directly or indirectly:
- Achieve or attempt to achieve any **undue gain or advantage** to themselves or associates
- Cause or attempt to cause any **undue loss** to company

**Examples**:
❌ Insider trading (director trades shares based on unpublished price-sensitive information)
❌ Director awards contract to own company at inflated prices
❌ Director receives commission/kickback from third party (without disclosure)
❌ Director diverts company's business to own entity

**Penalty** (Section 166(7)):
- **Liable to company** for:
  - Restitution of gain made
  - Compensation for loss caused to company
- **Fine**: ₹1 lakh - ₹5 lakh
- **Imprisonment**: Up to 1 year (if fraudulent intent)

---

### Secret Profits

**Rule**: Director cannot retain **secret profit** (profit made through directorship without company's consent).

**Examples**:
- Director receives commission from supplier (for awarding contract to supplier) → Secret profit (must disgorge to company)
- Director receives shares from acquirer (as inducement to support merger) → Secret profit (unless disclosed + approved by board)

**Remedy**: Company can:
- Sue director for **account of profits** (director must pay profit to company)
- **Rescind contract** (if secret commission/bribe tainted transaction)

---

## 7. Business Judgment Rule (Expanded)

### Application in India

**Indian Courts** recognize business judgment rule, but less developed than US Delaware law.

**Key Cases**:

**Tata Consultancy Services v. State of Andhra Pradesh (2004)**
- Supreme Court held: Courts should not interfere with **commercial decisions** of companies/directors
- Directors have discretion to make business decisions (courts not substitute their judgment)

**Principles**:
1. Directors are elected by shareholders to manage company (expertise presumed)
2. Courts lack business expertise (should not second-guess commercial decisions)
3. Risk-taking inherent in business (directors not liable for honest errors)

**Limitations**:
- Business judgment rule does NOT protect:
  - Fraud, self-dealing, bad faith
  - Gross negligence (reckless decisions, no reasonable basis)
  - Conflict of interest (directors personally benefited)
  - Oppression of minority shareholders

---

## 8. Director Liability and Penalties

### Personal Liability Scenarios

#### a. Section 339: Fraudulent Conduct

**Liability**: If company's business conducted with **intent to defraud creditors/members**, directors **personally liable** for company's debts.

**Elements**:
1. Company's business conducted fraudulently
2. Director **knew** OR ought to have known fraudulent conduct
3. Director actively participated OR turned blind eye

**Penalty**:
- **Imprisonment**: Up to 10 years
- **Fine**: ₹20 lakh - ₹300 crore (or 3 times defrauded amount, whichever higher)
- **Personal Liability**: Director liable for company's debts (corporate veil lifted)

**Example**:
- Company takes deposits from public (promising high returns)
- Company uses deposits to fund promoters' personal expenses (Ponzi scheme)
- Company insolvent, depositors unpaid
- **Directors personally liable** for depositors' losses (fraud u/s 339)

---

#### b. Section 447: Fraud

**Definition** (Section 447): Fraud includes any act, omission, concealment of fact, abuse of position:
- With intent to deceive company, members, creditors, public
- To gain undue advantage
- To injure interests of company, members, creditors, public

**Penalty**:
- **Imprisonment**: 6 months - 10 years
- **Fine**: ≥₹50 lakh (may extend to 3 times profit OR loss caused)
- **Disqualification**: Disqualified as director for 5 years (Section 164)

**Examples of Fraud**:
- Falsifying financial statements (inflating assets, concealing liabilities)
- Siphoning company funds (related party transactions at unfair prices)
- Insider trading (trading on UPSI - Unpublished Price Sensitive Information)

---

#### c. Criminal Liability (Other Offenses)

**Section 628**: Misapplication of company's property
- Director uses company property/money for personal benefit
- Imprisonment: Up to 5 years + fine

**Section 629**: Retaining wrongful gains
- Director retains property obtained wrongfully
- Imprisonment: Up to 2 years + fine

**IPC Offenses**: Directors can be prosecuted under:
- **Section 405-409 IPC**: Criminal breach of trust (director misappropriates company assets)
- **Section 420 IPC**: Cheating (director defrauds company/shareholders)
- **Section 409 IPC**: Criminal breach of trust by public servant/agent (applicable to directors)

---

### Vicarious Liability (Officer in Default)

**Concept**: If company commits offense (e.g., non-filing of returns), **every officer in default** liable.

**Officer in Default** (Section 2(60)) includes:
- Managing director, whole-time director
- Director charged with responsibility for compliance
- Any director who knowingly permitted non-compliance

**Example**:
- Company fails to file annual return (Section 92)
- **Company penalty**: ₹100/day (max ₹5 lakh)
- **Every officer in default penalty**: ₹100/day (max ₹5 lakh)
- Typically: MD, CEO, CFO, Company Secretary liable (if they were responsible for filing)

**Defense**: Officer can avoid liability if:
- They did not know about default (lacked knowledge)
- They exercised due diligence to prevent default
- Default occurred despite their efforts

---

## 9. Lifting of Corporate Veil

### General Principle: Limited Liability

**Salomon v. Salomon & Co (1897)**: Company is separate legal entity. Shareholders/directors enjoy **limited liability** (not personally liable for company's debts).

**Corporate Veil**: Legal separation between company and its members/directors.

---

### When Veil Lifted (Directors Personally Liable)

**Statutory Provisions**:

**1. Section 339 (Fraudulent Trading)**:
- If business conducted to defraud creditors → Directors personally liable

**2. Section 7(7) (Holding-Subsidiary Relationship)**:
- Holding company liable for subsidiary's debts (in certain circumstances)

**3. Section 399 (Oppression and Mismanagement)**:
- NCLT can lift veil if directors acted oppressively

**Judicial Grounds** (Common Law):

**a. Fraud or Improper Conduct**:
- Company used as façade to commit fraud
- Example: Director forms company to evade tax, conducts illegal business
- Court lifts veil, holds director personally liable

**b. Agency**:
- Company acts as agent of director/parent company
- Example: Subsidiary has no independent existence, merely acts as parent's agent
- Parent company liable for subsidiary's acts

**c. Group Enterprise**:
- Multiple companies in group treated as single economic entity
- Veil lifted to prevent corporate structure abuse

**d. Evasion of Legal Obligations**:
- Company formed to evade legal obligations (tax, contractual, statutory)
- Court lifts veil, looks at substance over form

**Landmark Case: Dinshaw Maneckjee Petit (1927)**
**Facts**: Shareholder held majority shares in multiple companies. Treated companies as personal property, intermingled funds.
**Held**: Corporate veil lifted. Shareholder personally liable for companies' debts (companies used as alter ego).

---

## 10. Directors & Officers (D&O) Insurance

### Purpose

**D&O Insurance** protects directors/officers from **personal liability** for acts done in capacity as director/officer.

**Coverage**:
- Legal defense costs (lawyers, court fees)
- Settlements, judgments (if director held liable)
- Investigation costs (regulatory inquiries, shareholder suits)

**Exclusions** (Typically):
- **Fraud**: Intentional fraud, dishonesty not covered
- **Personal Gain**: Claims arising from director's personal profit
- **Criminal Penalties**: Fines, penalties for criminal acts not covered
- **Known Claims**: Claims known at policy inception

---

### Why D&O Insurance Important

**High-Risk Areas for Director Liability**:
- Securities litigation (shareholder class actions for stock price drop)
- Regulatory investigations (SEBI, ROC, CCI, RBI)
- Employment disputes (wrongful termination, discrimination)
- M&A disputes (breach of fiduciary duty in sale/merger)
- Bankruptcy (wrongful trading, preference payments)

**Cost of Defense**: ₹50 lakh - ₹5 crore+ (even if director ultimately not liable)

**D&O Insurance**: Transfers this risk to insurer (company pays premium, insurer covers director's defense costs + liability).

---

### Section 197(13): Company Can Indemnify Directors

**Section 197(13)**: Company can purchase D&O insurance OR indemnify directors for liability incurred in discharge of duty (except fraud, willful default).

**Indemnification Clauses** (in AOA):
- Company indemnifies directors to fullest extent permitted by law
- Covers: Legal costs, settlements, judgments (for acts in good faith)
- Excludes: Fraud, willful misconduct, acts causing loss to company

---

## Best Practices for Directors

### Risk Mitigation

✅ **Informed Decisions**: Review board materials thoroughly, ask questions, seek expert advice (lawyers, CAs, consultants)
✅ **Board Minutes**: Ensure board minutes accurately reflect deliberations, dissents (if director disagrees with decision, record dissent)
✅ **Disclosure**: Disclose all conflicts of interest (Form MBP-1), abstain from voting on conflicted transactions
✅ **Attendance**: Attend board meetings regularly (≥75% attendance benchmark), participate actively
✅ **D&O Insurance**: Ensure company has adequate D&O insurance (coverage ≥₹50 crore for listed companies)
✅ **Compliance**: Monitor compliance (annual return, financial statements, tax filings, regulatory approvals)
✅ **Red Flags**: Raise concerns about red flags (deteriorating financials, related party transactions, non-compliance)

---

### When to Dissent

**Director Should Dissent If**:
- Decision violates law, regulations, AOA
- Decision involves conflict of interest (self-dealing by other directors)
- Decision lacks reasonable basis (arbitrary, irrational)
- Insufficient information provided (board asked to decide without adequate materials)

**How to Dissent**:
- State dissent **during board meeting** (voice dissent, request recorded in minutes)
- If not present at meeting, send written dissent within 7 days of receiving minutes
- Consider resigning if repeated dissents ignored (continuing as director may imply acquiescence)

---

### When to Seek Legal Advice

**Seek Legal Advice Before**:
- Major transactions (M&A, capital raising, asset sales >10% of company's assets)
- Related party transactions (involving directors, promoters, their relatives)
- Regulatory investigations (SEBI, RBI, CCI, ROC)
- Shareholder disputes (oppression claims, derivative suits)
- Material non-compliance (missed filings, tax defaults)

---

## Conclusion

Directors owe comprehensive **fiduciary duties** to company - duty of loyalty, duty of care, duty to act in good faith, duty to avoid conflicts.

**Key Takeaways**:

1. ✅ **Section 166**: Codifies fiduciary duties (good faith, care/skill/diligence, avoid conflicts, no undue gain)
2. ✅ **Business Judgment Rule**: Protects directors for honest errors IF decision made in good faith, on informed basis, without conflict
3. ✅ **No-Profit Rule**: Directors cannot retain profit made through directorship (Regal Hastings case)
4. ✅ **Disclosure**: Mandatory disclosure of interests (Section 184), non-interested directors approve transactions
5. ✅ **Personal Liability**: Directors personally liable for fraud (Section 339), criminal breach of trust, wrongful trading
6. ✅ **Lifting Veil**: Corporate veil lifted for fraud, improper conduct, evasion of legal obligations
7. ✅ **D&O Insurance**: Essential risk mitigation (covers legal costs, settlements for acts in good faith)

**Strategic Advice**:

- **For Directors**: Attend meetings, stay informed, disclose conflicts, dissent when appropriate, ensure D&O insurance, seek legal advice for major decisions
- **For Companies**: Conduct board training (fiduciary duties, compliance), maintain D&O insurance (≥₹50 crore for listed), document board deliberations thoroughly
- **For Shareholders**: Monitor director performance (attendance, related party transactions), use oppression remedy (Section 241) if directors breach duties

---

**Next Protocols to Consult**:
- **IL-CORP-BOARD-MEETINGS**: Board procedures, quorum, voting, minutes
- **IL-CORP-RELATED-PARTY-TRANSACTIONS**: RPT disclosure and approval
- **IL-CORP-AUDIT-COMMITTEE**: Audit committee oversight
- **CIVIC-TRIBUNALS**: NCLT for director liability claims

---

**Legal Disclaimer**: This protocol provides informational guidance on director duties in India. It is NOT legal advice. For specific director liability issues, breach of duty claims, or D&O insurance, engage a qualified Corporate Lawyer. Legal liability depends on facts, evidence, business judgment rule application, and NCLT/court adjudication.

---

**End of Protocol: IL-CORP-DIRECTOR-DUTIES**


---
## IL-CORP-DIRECTORS-APPOINTMENT.md
---

# IL-CORP-DIRECTORS-APPOINTMENT
## Director Appointment, Qualifications, and Removal

---
id: IL-CORP-DIRECTORS-APPOINTMENT
version: 1.0.0
category: corporate_law
subcategory: board_management
sections:
  - Director Identification Number (DIN)
  - Types of Directors
  - Qualifications and Disqualifications
  - Appointment Procedure
  - Tenure and Retirement
  - Removal of Directors
  - Resignation of Directors
dependencies:
  - IL-CORP-MOA-AOA (Directors provisions in AOA)
  - IL-COMPANIES-ACT (Companies Act 2013, Sections 149-172)
related_protocols:
  foundation:
    - IL-CORP-DIRECTOR-DUTIES (Director fiduciary duties)
    - IL-CORP-BOARD-MEETINGS (Board meeting procedures)
  remedies:
    - CIVIC-WRIT-BASICS (Writ against illegal removal)
    - CIVIC-TRIBUNALS (NCLT for director disputes)
landmark_cases:
  - Shanti Prasad Jain v. Kalinga Tubes AIR 1965 SC 1535 (Director removal procedure)
  - Needle Industries v. Needle Industries Newey AIR 1981 SC 1298 (Independent directors)
  - Maneka Gandhi v. Union of India AIR 1978 SC 597 (Natural justice in removal)
---

## Overview

**Director** = Person appointed to the board of directors to manage company affairs (Section 2(34)).

**Legal Framework**:
- Companies Act 2013: Sections 149-172 (Directors)
- Companies (Appointment and Qualification of Directors) Rules 2014
- SEBI (Listing Obligations and Disclosure Requirements) Regulations 2015 (for listed companies)

**Key Principle**: Directors owe **fiduciary duties** to company (duty of care, loyalty, act in good faith). Directors act as agents of company, not shareholders.

---

## Director Identification Number (DIN)

### Section 153: DIN Mandatory

**Definition**: DIN = Unique identification number issued by Central Government to every person intending to be appointed as director.

**Purpose**:
- Track directors across multiple companies
- Prevent disqualified persons from becoming directors
- Facilitate ROC compliance monitoring

**Characteristics**:
- Permanent (once allotted, valid for life)
- Unique (one person, one DIN)
- Portable (applies across all companies where person is director)

---

### DIN Application Procedure

**Form**: DIR-3 (Application for allotment of DIN)

**Documents Required**:
- PAN card
- Aadhaar card (for Indian nationals) OR Passport (for foreign nationals)
- Proof of identity + address
- Photograph
- Email ID, mobile number

**Processing Time**: 1-3 days (if all documents correct)

**Fee**: NIL (free)

**Validity**: Permanent (no renewal required)

**DIN Deactivation** (Rule 10):
- If director doesn't file financial statements/annual return for **3 consecutive years** across ALL companies
- MCA deactivates DIN
- Reactivation: File Form DIR-3C (KYC) + pending ROC filings

---

## Types of Directors

### 1. Executive Directors

**Definition**: Directors involved in day-to-day management, holding executive position (CEO, MD, CFO, CTO, COO).

**Key Features**:
✅ Full-time employees of company (employment contract)
✅ Draw salary, allowances, benefits (as per contract)
✅ Report to board, but have operational authority
✅ Subject to board oversight, performance review

**Appointment**: Board resolution + employment contract + shareholder approval (if MD/whole-time director)

**Tenure**: Typically 3-5 years (renewable)

**Examples**:
- **Managing Director (MD)**: Chief executive, overall charge of business operations (Section 2(54))
- **Chief Executive Officer (CEO)**: Top management, strategic decisions
- **Chief Financial Officer (CFO)**: Financial management, accounts, compliance
- **Whole-Time Director (WTD)**: Full-time director, specific portfolio (sales, operations, HR)

---

### 2. Non-Executive Directors

**Definition**: Directors NOT involved in day-to-day management, attend board meetings, provide strategic guidance.

**Key Features**:
✅ Part-time (no employment contract, only directorship)
✅ Receive sitting fees (per board meeting attended) + commission (if approved)
✅ Provide oversight, strategic guidance
✅ No operational authority (only board-level decisions)

**Appointment**: Board resolution + shareholder approval (ordinary resolution)

**Examples**:
- Promoter/shareholder directors (founders, investors)
- Industry experts, advisors
- Government nominees (in PSUs)

---

### 3. Independent Directors (Section 149(5))

**Definition**: Directors independent of management, no material/pecuniary relationship with company/promoters.

**Criteria for Independence** (Section 149(6)):
- ✅ NOT promoter or related to promoter/director
- ✅ NOT employed by company (current or past 3 years)
- ✅ NOT relative of director/KMP
- ✅ NOT material business relationship with company (>2% revenue OR ₹50 lakh, whichever lower)
- ✅ NOT partner/executive of company's auditor (current or past 3 years)
- ✅ NOT holding >2% equity shares
- ✅ NOT nominee director (appointed by institution/lender)

**Mandatory For** (Section 149(4)):
- **Listed companies**: Min 1/3rd board must be independent directors
- **Public companies** (paid-up capital ≥₹10 crore OR turnover ≥₹100 crore OR outstanding loans/debentures ≥₹50 crore): Min 2 independent directors

**Tenure** (Section 149(10)):
- Max 2 consecutive terms of 5 years each (total 10 years)
- After 10 years, cannot be reappointed for next 3 years
- Can be reappointed after 3-year cooling-off period

**Appointment Procedure**:
1. Board identifies potential independent director candidates
2. Nomination & Remuneration Committee (NRC) recommends candidate
3. Board passes resolution proposing appointment
4. Shareholders approve via **special resolution** (75% majority) - Section 149(7)
5. Independent director files declaration of independence (Form DIR-8)

**Removal**: Only via special resolution (75% majority) + opportunity to be heard

**Role**:
- Oversight and governance (not management)
- Member of audit committee, NRC, stakeholders relationship committee (mandatory for listed companies)
- Independent judgment on strategy, performance, resources, standards of conduct

---

### 4. Nominee Directors

**Definition**: Directors nominated by specific institutions/lenders (banks, financial institutions, investors).

**Appointment**: Per nomination rights in loan agreement, SHA, or statute (e.g., government nominee in PSUs).

**Examples**:
- Bank nominee director (in companies with large borrowings)
- Investor nominee director (per SHA)
- Government nominee (in PSUs, Section 8 companies)

**Fiduciary Duty**: Despite being nominee, owes duty to **company** (not just nominating entity) - *Scottish Co-operative Wholesale Society v. Meyer* [1959] AC 324

---

### 5. Alternate Directors (Section 161(2))

**Definition**: Director appointed to act in place of original director during their absence (≥3 months).

**Appointment**: Board resolution (if AOA permits)

**Tenure**: Till original director returns (max duration = original director's absence period)

**Powers**: Same as original director (attend board meetings, vote)

**Use Case**: Original director on long overseas assignment, medical leave

---

### 6. Additional Directors (Section 161(1))

**Definition**: Directors appointed by board between two general meetings (to fill casual vacancy OR increase board strength).

**Appointment**: Board resolution (if AOA permits)

**Tenure**: Till next AGM (at AGM, shareholders elect directors)

**Use Case**: Urgent need for director (e.g., investor nominee director after funding round closes, cannot wait for next AGM)

---

### 7. Small Shareholders' Director (Section 151)

**Definition**: Director elected by small shareholders (holding ≤₹20,000 shares OR ≤ such amount prescribed).

**Mandatory For**: Listed companies OR public companies with ≥1,000 small shareholders

**Election**: By special resolution of small shareholders (not all shareholders)

**Tenure**: 3 years (can be reelected)

**Purpose**: Represent small shareholders' interests on board

---

## Qualifications and Disqualifications

### Minimum Age (Section 196(3))

**Minimum Age**: 18 years

**No Maximum Age** (for private companies)

**Public Companies**: Directors aged >75 years require **special resolution** for appointment (Section 196(3))

---

### Residency Requirement (Section 149(3))

**Mandatory**: At least **1 director** must be resident in India (stayed in India for ≥182 days in previous calendar year)

**Purpose**: Ensure Indian presence, regulatory compliance

---

### Number of Directorships (Section 165)

**Maximum Limit**:
- **20 companies** total (including private + public companies)
- Out of 20, max **10 public companies**

**Exclusions** (not counted):
- Alternate directorships
- Directorship in dormant companies
- Private companies (Section 8 companies, wholly owned subsidiaries)

**Penalty for Excess**: ₹5,000 per additional directorship (beyond limit)

---

### Independent Director Limit (Section 165)

**Maximum**: Independent director in max **7 listed companies**

**If Executive Director in ANY Listed Company**: Independent director in max **3 OTHER listed companies**

**Purpose**: Prevent over-commitment, ensure effective oversight

---

### Disqualifications (Section 164)

**Disqualified from Appointment**:

**1. Undischarged Insolvent** (Section 164(1)(a)):
- Person adjudicated insolvent, insolvency proceedings pending
- Disqualified till discharge from insolvency

**2. Convicted of Fraud/Moral Turpitude** (Section 164(1)(b)):
- Convicted and sentenced to ≥6 months imprisonment
- Disqualified for **5 years** from conviction date + sentence period

**3. Non-Payment of Calls** (Section 164(1)(c)):
- Failed to pay calls on shares (within 6 months of due date)
- Disqualified till calls paid

**4. Non-Filing of Financial Statements** (Section 164(2)):
- Company (where person is director) failed to file financial statements/annual return for **3 consecutive financial years**
- Disqualified in **ALL companies** (not just defaulting company)
- Disqualified for **5 years** from end of 3rd financial year

**5. Disqualified by Court/Tribunal** (Section 164(1)(d)):
- Court/NCLT order disqualifying person (for fraud, mismanagement)
- Disqualified for period specified in order

**Verification**: Company must verify DIN on MCA portal before appointing director (check if disqualified).

**Penalty**: If company appoints disqualified person:
- Company: ₹1 lakh - ₹5 lakh fine
- Officer in default: ₹50,000 - ₹5 lakh fine

---

## Appointment Procedure

### First Directors (Section 152(1))

**Appointment**: Named in MOA OR Articles of Association

**Tenure**: Till first AGM (at first AGM, shareholders elect directors)

**No Board/Shareholder Approval Needed**: Pre-incorporated appointment (subscribers sign MOA agreeing to first directors)

---

### Subsequent Directors

#### 1. Appointment in General Meeting (Section 152(2))

**Procedure**:
1. **Board Proposes Candidate**:
   - Board identifies candidate
   - NRC (if mandatory) recommends candidate
   - Board resolution proposing candidate for shareholder approval

2. **Notice to Shareholders** (21 days before general meeting):
   - Agenda: Appointment of [Name] as director
   - Explanatory statement (Section 102): Candidate's background, expertise, directorships

3. **Shareholders Vote**:
   - **Ordinary Resolution** (simple majority) for non-independent directors
   - **Special Resolution** (75% majority) for independent directors

4. **Director Consents** (Form DIR-2):
   - Candidate files consent to act as director
   - Declaration: Not disqualified u/s 164, complies with DIN requirements

5. **ROC Filing** (within 30 days):
   - Form DIR-12: Notice of appointment
   - Documents: Board resolution, consent, special resolution (if independent director)

---

#### 2. Appointment by Board (Section 161)

**When Allowed**:
- Casual vacancy (director death, resignation, removal)
- Additional director (increase board strength between AGMs)
- If AOA permits

**Procedure**:
1. Board resolution appointing director
2. Director files Form DIR-2 (consent)
3. ROC filing: Form DIR-12 (within 30 days)

**Tenure**: Till next AGM (at AGM, shareholders must approve appointment via ordinary resolution)

---

### Director's Consent and Disclosures

**Form DIR-2**: Consent to Act as Director
- Candidate's details (name, DIN, address)
- Declaration: Not disqualified, complies with directorships limit
- Consent to act as director

**Form DIR-8**: Declaration by Independent Director
- Declaration of independence (meets Section 149(6) criteria)
- Filed annually (at first board meeting of financial year)

**Form MBP-1**: Disclosure of Interest by Director (Section 184)
- Director discloses interests in other companies/LLPs/firms
- Filed at first board meeting + updated when interests change

---

## Tenure and Retirement

### Managing Director / Whole-Time Director (Section 196)

**Maximum Tenure**: 5 years per appointment (can be reappointed)

**Cooling-Off**: No cooling-off period required (can be immediately reappointed after 5 years)

**Shareholder Approval**: Ordinary resolution for appointment/reappointment

---

### Retirement by Rotation (Section 152)

**Applicability**: **Public companies** only (not private companies, unless AOA provides)

**Rule**: 2/3rd of directors (excluding independent directors, MD/WTD) retire by rotation

**Rotation Schedule**:
- At every AGM, 1/3rd of rotational directors retire
- Longest-serving directors retire first (FIFO)
- Retiring directors eligible for reelection

**Example**:
- Board: 6 directors (2 independent, 1 MD, 3 rotational directors)
- Rotational: 3 directors (2/3rd of 6 - 2 independent - 1 MD = 3)
- At AGM: 1 director retires (3 / 3 years = 1 per year)

**Exemption**: Directors appointed via special resolution exempted from retirement by rotation (Section 152(6))

---

## Removal of Directors

### 1. Removal by Shareholders (Section 169)

**Grounds**: Any reason (with or without cause)

**Procedure**:

**Step 1: Special Notice** (14 days before general meeting):
- Shareholder(s) intending to remove director must give **special notice** to company (14 days before meeting)
- Special notice can be given by **any shareholder** (no minimum shareholding required)

**Step 2: Company Sends Notice to Director**:
- Company forwards copy of special notice to director (whose removal proposed)
- Director has right to make representations (written submission)

**Step 3: Director's Right to Be Heard**:
- Director entitled to attend general meeting
- Director can speak, defend against removal
- Director's written representations circulated to shareholders (if received 7 days before meeting)

**Step 4: Shareholders Vote** (Ordinary Resolution):
- Simple majority (>50%) required to remove director
- Director (whose removal proposed) can vote on resolution (if shareholder)

**Step 5: ROC Filing** (within 30 days):
- Form DIR-12: Notice of cessation
- Copy of ordinary resolution

**Compensation** (Section 202):
- If director removed before tenure ends, entitled to compensation for **breach of employment contract**
- Compensation = Remaining salary + benefits as per employment contract
- Not applicable if removal for cause (fraud, gross misconduct)

**Landmark Case: Shanti Prasad Jain v. Kalinga Tubes (1965)**
**Facts**: Director removed without opportunity to be heard.
**Held**: Natural justice requires opportunity to be heard (even though statute doesn't explicitly mandate). Director's right to attend meeting, speak, make representations is implied.

---

### 2. Removal by NCLT (Sections 241, 242)

**Grounds**: Oppression and mismanagement, class action suits

**Procedure**: NCLT petition by minority shareholders → NCLT hearing → NCLT order removing director

**Example**: Director diverting company funds → Minority shareholders file oppression petition → NCLT removes director

---

### 3. Automatic Vacation of Office (Section 167)

**Director's Office Automatically Vacated If**:

**a. Disqualification** (Section 164):
- Becomes undischarged insolvent, convicted of fraud, non-payment of calls, non-filing for 3 years

**b. Absence from Board Meetings** (Section 167(1)(b)):
- Absent from **all board meetings** for 12 months (with or without leave)
- Board can grant leave of absence (prevents automatic vacation)

**c. Resignation Not Accepted** (Section 168(1)):
- Director files resignation, company doesn't accept/reject within 30 days
- Resignation takes effect after 30 days (automatic vacation)

**d. Acts as Director Despite Disqualification**:
- Penalty: ₹1 lakh - ₹5 lakh fine + imprisonment up to 1 year (Section 167(2))

**e. Non-Disclosure of Interest** (Section 184):
- Repeatedly fails to disclose interests in contracts

---

## Resignation of Directors

### Section 168: Resignation Procedure

**Step 1: Director Submits Resignation**:
- Written resignation letter to company
- Effective date (immediate OR future date)

**Step 2: Company Board Meeting** (within 30 days):
- Board considers resignation
- Board can **accept** OR **reject** (if resignation harms company, e.g., all directors resigning simultaneously)

**Step 3: Resignation Takes Effect**:
- **If accepted**: Effective from acceptance date
- **If rejected**: Director can approach NCLT (Section 168(4)) for relief
- **If no response within 30 days**: Resignation deemed accepted, effective after 30 days

**Step 4: ROC Filing** (within 30 days of cessation):
- Form DIR-12: Notice of cessation
- Copy of resignation letter

**Director's Liability After Resignation**:
- Liability for acts done during tenure (not retrospectively extinguished)
- Example: Director approved related party transaction (violating law) → Resigned → Still liable for that act

---

### Director's Right to Resign

**General Rule**: Director has right to resign anytime (no permission needed)

**Exception** (Section 168(2)):
- If resignation results in **all directors resigning** (board becomes vacant), resigning directors must call general meeting **before resignation takes effect**
- Purpose: Ensure continuity (shareholders elect new directors before old directors resign)

---

## Best Practices

### Director Appointment

✅ **Due Diligence**: Verify DIN, check disqualifications on MCA portal before appointing
✅ **Background Check**: Verify candidate's professional background, past directorships, litigation history
✅ **Conflict of Interest Check**: Ensure candidate has no material conflict with company (competitors, related parties)
✅ **Independent Directors**: For listed companies, ensure robust selection process (NRC recommendation, special resolution)
✅ **Diversity**: Consider gender diversity (at least 1 woman director for prescribed companies - Section 149(1)), skill diversity (finance, operations, legal, tech)

---

### Director Tenure Management

✅ **Retirement by Rotation Schedule**: Maintain rotation schedule (for public companies), ensure retiring directors are reelected (if desired)
✅ **Succession Planning**: Identify potential directors in advance (for retirement, resignation, expansion)
✅ **Performance Review**: Annual board evaluation (assess each director's performance, attendance, contributions)

---

### Director Removal/Resignation

✅ **Exit Interviews**: Understand reasons for resignation (improve governance)
✅ **Smooth Transition**: Ensure knowledge transfer, handover (if executive director)
✅ **Communication**: Announce departure to stakeholders (investors, employees, regulators)
✅ **Avoid Simultaneous Resignations**: Stagger resignations (prevent board vacancy)

---

## Conclusion

Director appointment, tenure, and removal are governed by Companies Act 2013 with emphasis on governance, independence, and shareholder protection.

**Key Takeaways**:

1. ✅ **DIN Mandatory**: Every director must have DIN (permanent, unique identifier)
2. ✅ **Types**: Executive (day-to-day management), non-executive (oversight), independent (1/3rd for listed companies)
3. ✅ **Disqualifications**: Insolvency, fraud conviction, non-filing for 3 years, non-payment of calls
4. ✅ **Appointment**: Shareholders elect (ordinary resolution for non-independent, special resolution for independent)
5. ✅ **Removal**: Ordinary resolution (simple majority) with special notice (14 days), director has right to be heard
6. ✅ **Resignation**: Director can resign anytime, takes effect after 30 days if not accepted/rejected

**Strategic Advice**:

- **For Companies**: Appoint independent directors early (avoid last-minute compliance), maintain director diversity (skills, gender), conduct annual board evaluations
- **For Directors**: File DIN KYC annually (avoid deactivation), disclose interests (Section 184), attend board meetings (avoid automatic vacation for absence)
- **For Shareholders**: Use Section 169 removal for underperforming/conflicted directors, ensure special notice (14 days) for removal

---

**Next Protocols to Consult**:
- **IL-CORP-DIRECTOR-DUTIES**: Fiduciary duties, duty of care, duty of loyalty
- **IL-CORP-BOARD-MEETINGS**: Board meeting procedures, quorum, voting
- **IL-CORP-RELATED-PARTY-TRANSACTIONS**: Disclosure and approval of RPTs
- **CIVIC-TRIBUNALS**: NCLT for director disputes

---

**Legal Disclaimer**: This protocol provides informational guidance on director appointment in India. It is NOT legal advice. For specific director appointment, removal, or disputes, engage a qualified Company Secretary or Corporate Lawyer. Legal compliance depends on Companies Act 2013, AOA provisions, and NCLT/court procedures.

---

**End of Protocol: IL-CORP-DIRECTORS-APPOINTMENT**
