## IL-IBC-PERSONAL-GUARANTORS.md
---

# Personal Guarantors Under IBC - Section 60 Joint Insolvency Framework

## Protocol ID
**IL-IBC-PERSONAL-GUARANTORS** (v1.0)

---

## Purpose
Guide creditors, personal guarantors, and insolvency professionals through the joint insolvency framework under Section 60 of the Insolvency and Bankruptcy Code 2016, covering corporate debtor default → personal guarantor liability, application process, asset realization, repayment plan structuring, and discharge upon completion.

---

## Legal Framework

### 1. Section 60 - Joint Insolvency Proceedings

**Statutory Provision** (Section 60):
```
"Where a corporate insolvency resolution process or liquidation proceeding
of a corporate debtor is pending before the NCLT, an application relating
to the insolvency resolution or bankruptcy of a corporate guarantor or
personal guarantor, as the case may be, of such corporate debtor shall
be filed before the NCLT."
```

**Key Principles**:
- **Joint Liability**: Personal guarantor liable for corporate debtor's default (Indian Contract Act 1872, Section 128)
- **Simultaneous Proceedings**: Creditor can file against personal guarantor DURING or AFTER corporate debtor CIRP/liquidation
- **Same NCLT Bench**: Both proceedings heard by same bench (territorial jurisdiction where corporate debtor's registered office located)
- **Independent Timelines**: Personal guarantor CIRP runs 180-330 days independently of corporate debtor proceedings

### 2. Personal Guarantor Definition

**Who Qualifies**:
1. **Individual Guarantor**: Natural person who executes personal guarantee for corporate debtor's obligations
2. **Joint & Several Liability**: Multiple guarantors jointly liable (creditor can pursue any one for full amount)
3. **Continuing Guarantee**: Covers all transactions during guarantee period (not limited to specific transaction)

**Exclusions**:
- **Corporate Guarantor**: Separate company providing guarantee → treated as corporate debtor under Part II of IBC
- **Collateral Provider**: Individual who pledges security but doesn't execute guarantee deed (not personally liable beyond collateral)

### 3. Applicable Threshold

**Minimum Default Amount** (Section 4, amended 2020):
- **₹1,000 minimum**: Personal guarantor proceedings can be initiated for debts ≥₹1,000 (reduced from ₹1 lakh to widen coverage)
- **Aggregate Debt**: If multiple creditors, aggregate debt across all creditors must be ≥₹1,000

**Practical Reality**:
- Most personal guarantee cases involve corporate borrowings ≥₹1 crore (banks typically don't pursue small guarantees due to cost)
- High net-worth guarantors targeted (promoters/directors with substantial personal assets)

---

## Application Process Under Part III (Sections 94-100)

### Step 1: Application Filing by Creditor

**Who Can File** (Section 95):
1. **Financial Creditor**: Bank/NBFC with financial debt owed by corporate debtor + guarantee by individual
2. **Operational Creditor**: Supplier/service provider with operational debt ≥₹1,000 + personal guarantee
3. **Personal Guarantor Himself**: Can file voluntary application under Section 95 (admit inability to pay debts)

**Documentation Required**:
1. **Guarantee Deed**: Original/certified copy showing personal guarantee execution
2. **Corporate Debtor Default**: NCLT order admitting corporate debtor under CIRP/liquidation OR default notice issued ≥14 days prior
3. **Debt Amount**: Statement showing outstanding principal + interest + costs
4. **Personal Assets Statement**: Affidavit disclosing guarantor's assets (property, investments, income sources)

**Application Format** (Form 5 under IBC Insolvency Rules):
```
IN THE NATIONAL COMPANY LAW TRIBUNAL
[BENCH LOCATION] BENCH

COMPANY APPLICATION NO. ___ OF 2025
(Under Section 95 of the Insolvency and Bankruptcy Code, 2016)

In the matter of: ABC Manufacturing Ltd. (Corporate Debtor)
AND
In the matter of: Mr. Rajesh Kumar (Personal Guarantor)

APPLICANT: State Bank of India (Financial Creditor)

FACTS:
1. Corporate debtor ABC Manufacturing Ltd. admitted under CIRP vide NCLT
   order dated 15.03.2025 (CP No. 245/2024)
2. Outstanding debt: ₹150 crore (term loan) + ₹18 crore (interest)
3. Personal guarantee executed by Mr. Rajesh Kumar (Director & Promoter)
   on 10.01.2020 (Annexure A)
4. Guarantor's personal assets: ₹75 crore estimated (Annexure B)

RELIEF SOUGHT:
a) Admit personal guarantor under insolvency resolution process
b) Appoint Resolution Professional to manage guarantor's assets
c) Moratorium on guarantor's personal assets during proceedings
```

### Step 2: NCLT Admission

**Admission Criteria** (Section 96):
1. **Guarantee Existence**: Valid personal guarantee deed executed by individual
2. **Corporate Debtor Default**: Corporate debtor under CIRP/liquidation OR debt due and payable
3. **Debt Threshold**: ≥₹1,000 (practical cases ≥₹1 crore)
4. **No Pre-existing Proceedings**: Personal guarantor not already under insolvency proceedings

**Timeline**:
- **14 days**: NCLT must admit or reject application within 14 days of filing (Section 96(2))
- **Rejection Grounds**: Incomplete application, guarantee not valid, debt disputed on substantial grounds

**Admission Order**:
```
ORDER

1. Application admitted under Section 95 read with Section 60.
2. Mr. Rajesh Kumar (personal guarantor) admitted under insolvency
   resolution process.
3. Ms. Priya Sharma (IBBI Reg. No. IBBI/IPA-001/IP-P00123/2018-19/10234)
   appointed as Resolution Professional.
4. Moratorium declared under Section 96(1):
   - No suits against personal guarantor
   - No execution of decrees against guarantor's property
   - No alienation of guarantor's assets during moratorium
5. RP to submit list of guarantor's assets within 30 days.
6. Public announcement to be issued within 7 days (Form 6).
```

---

## Personal Guarantor Insolvency Process

### Phase 1: Asset Verification & Claims (Day 0-60)

**Day 0-7: Public Announcement**
- RP issues public announcement (Form 6) inviting claims from all creditors
- Published in: 2 newspapers (English + regional), RP's website, IBBI website

**Day 0-30: Asset Custody**
- RP takes custody of ALL personal assets:
  ```
  ASSET INVENTORY - Mr. Rajesh Kumar

  A. IMMOVABLE PROPERTY:
  1. Residential House, Juhu Mumbai: ₹45 crore (market value)
     - Self-occupied (family residence)
     - Mortgage: Nil (unencumbered)

  2. Farmhouse, Lonavala: ₹8 crore
     - Recreational property (not primary residence)
     - Mortgage: ₹2 crore (HDFC Bank)

  3. Commercial Office, Nariman Point: ₹12 crore
     - Rented to third party (₹15 lakh/month rent)
     - Mortgage: Nil

  B. MOVABLE ASSETS:
  1. Bank Accounts: ₹3.5 crore (SBI, HDFC, ICICI)
  2. Mutual Funds: ₹6 crore (equity + debt funds)
  3. Listed Stocks: ₹4 crore (demat account)
  4. Life Insurance: ₹2 crore (surrender value)
  5. Vehicles: ₹1.5 crore (Mercedes S-Class, BMW X5, Audi A6)

  C. INCOME SOURCES:
  1. Salary: ₹2 lakh/month (if employed elsewhere)
  2. Rental Income: ₹15 lakh/month (commercial office)
  3. Dividends: ₹50 lakh/year (stock holdings)

  TOTAL ASSETS: ₹82 crore (market value)
  TOTAL LIABILITIES: ₹168 crore (₹150cr corporate + ₹18cr interest)

  NET DEFICIT: ₹86 crore (assets insufficient to cover guarantee liability)
  ```

**Day 0-60: Claims Finalization**
- Creditors submit claims with proof (loan agreements, invoices, guarantee deeds)
- RP verifies claims and prepares final list
- **Claims Admitted**:
  ```
  CLAIMS STATEMENT

  A. FINANCIAL CREDITORS:
  1. State Bank of India: ₹168 crore (₹150cr principal + ₹18cr interest)
     - Basis: Personal guarantee dated 10.01.2020 (Annexure A)
     - Corporate debtor default: Yes (CIRP admitted 15.03.2025)

  B. SECURED CREDITORS:
  1. HDFC Bank: ₹2 crore (mortgage on Lonavala farmhouse)
     - Security: Farmhouse worth ₹8cr, shortfall secured against other assets

  C. OPERATIONAL CREDITORS:
  1. Income Tax Department: ₹35 lakh (personal income tax arrears)
  2. Credit Card Dues: ₹12 lakh (HDFC, Citibank cards)

  TOTAL CLAIMS: ₹170.47 crore
  ```

### Phase 2: Repayment Plan Submission (Day 60-180)

**Repayment Plan Structure** (Section 101):

Personal guarantor (or creditor/third party on guarantor's behalf) can submit repayment plan proposing:

1. **Payment Schedule**: Structured payments over 3-5 years from:
   - Liquidation of non-essential assets
   - Future income (salary, rental income, dividends)
   - Family support (spouse/children contributions)
   - Third-party funding (friends/relatives)

2. **Asset Retention**: Guarantor can propose retaining essential assets:
   - **Primary Residence**: One residential property for family (up to reasonable value ₹1-2 crore)
   - **Income-Generating Assets**: Rental property generating income to service repayment plan
   - **Tools of Trade**: If self-employed, tools/equipment necessary for livelihood

3. **Discharge**: Upon completion of repayment plan (3-5 years), guarantor discharged from ALL debts (including unpaid balance)

**Example Repayment Plan**:

```
REPAYMENT PLAN - Mr. Rajesh Kumar
(Submitted by Guarantor with family support)

A. ASSET LIQUIDATION (Year 1):
1. Farmhouse, Lonavala: ₹8cr sale → ₹2cr to HDFC Bank, ₹6cr to creditors
2. Commercial Office, Nariman Point: ₹12cr sale → creditors
3. Vehicles (2 out of 3): ₹1cr sale (retain 1 car for family use)
4. Mutual Funds: ₹6cr redemption → creditors
5. Listed Stocks: ₹4cr sale → creditors
6. Life Insurance: ₹2cr surrender → creditors

SUBTOTAL (Year 1): ₹31 crore realized

B. RETAINED ASSETS:
1. Residential House, Juhu: ₹45cr (RETAINED - family residence)
   - Rationale: Primary residence for guarantor + wife + 2 minor children
   - No alternative accommodation available
   - Creditors to take mortgage charge (realization upon guarantor's death
     or sale in future)

2. Bank Accounts: ₹50 lakh retained (₹3cr → creditors immediately,
   ₹50L retained for living expenses during 5-year plan)

3. One Vehicle: ₹50 lakh value (BMW X5 retained for family transport)

C. INCOME CONTRIBUTIONS (Year 1-5):
1. Salary: ₹2 lakh/month × 60 months = ₹1.2 crore
   - Guarantor currently unemployed, will secure employment (management
     consultant / advisory roles)
   - Living expenses: ₹1 lakh/month (₹60L over 5 years)
   - Contribution to plan: ₹1 lakh/month = ₹60L total

2. Rental Income: Juhu residence partial rental (if guarantor downsizes)
   - Current: 5 BHK (₹45cr value)
   - Plan: Rent out 2 floors (₹3 lakh/month), retain 1 floor for family
   - Contribution: ₹3 lakh/month × 60 months = ₹1.8 crore

3. Family Support: ₹2 crore over 5 years
   - Guarantor's brother (businessman): ₹1 crore contribution
   - Guarantor's father (retired, savings): ₹50 lakh
   - Guarantor's spouse (working professional): ₹50 lakh

D. TOTAL PAYMENT TO CREDITORS:
Year 1: ₹31 crore (asset liquidation)
Year 2-5: ₹4.6 crore (₹60L salary + ₹1.8cr rent + ₹2cr family)
TOTAL: ₹35.6 crore paid over 5 years

E. CREDITOR RECOVERY:
Total Claims: ₹170.47 crore
Total Payment: ₹35.6 crore
Recovery Rate: 20.9%

F. DISCHARGE:
Upon payment of ₹35.6 crore over 5 years, guarantor DISCHARGED from:
- Remaining ₹134.87 crore (₹170.47cr - ₹35.6cr)
- State Bank of India waives remaining ₹132.4 crore
- Other creditors waive remaining ₹2.47 crore

Guarantor gets FRESH START after 5 years, retains Juhu residence
(₹45cr, now unencumbered), can rebuild financial life.
```

**Repayment Plan Evaluation Criteria**:

1. **Maximization of Assets**: Does plan realize maximum value from guarantor's assets?
2. **Reasonableness**: Are proposed asset retentions reasonable (primary residence vs luxury assets)?
3. **Feasibility**: Can guarantor realistically generate proposed income (salary, rent)?
4. **Better than Liquidation**: Does plan offer creditors MORE than immediate liquidation of all assets?

**Creditor Vote**:
- **Committee of Creditors (CoC)**: All creditors with admitted claims vote
- **Approval Threshold**: ≥75% voting share (supermajority, higher than corporate CIRP's 66%)
- **Rejection**: If plan rejected, personal guarantor moves to bankruptcy (liquidation of ALL assets including primary residence)

### Phase 3: Plan Implementation (Year 1-5)

**Monitoring by RP**:
- RP continues as "Implementation Monitor" during 5-year plan
- Quarterly compliance reports to NCLT & creditors
- Guarantor must:
  - Sell specified assets within timelines (Year 1 asset sales completed)
  - Maintain employment/income sources (submit salary slips quarterly)
  - Make scheduled payments (quarterly installments: ₹2cr in Year 1, then ₹1.15cr/year for Years 2-5)

**Default on Plan**:
- If guarantor defaults on 2 consecutive quarterly payments → Plan terminated
- Bankruptcy declared → Immediate liquidation of ALL assets (including primary residence)
- No discharge (guarantor remains liable for unpaid debts even after asset liquidation)

**Successful Completion**:
- Year 5, final payment made: ₹35.6 crore paid total
- NCLT issues **Discharge Order**:
  ```
  ORDER OF DISCHARGE

  1. Repayment plan completed successfully (total payment: ₹35.6 crore).
  2. Mr. Rajesh Kumar DISCHARGED from all debts under Section 101(3).
  3. Remaining liabilities (₹134.87 crore) stand EXTINGUISHED.
  4. Guarantor free from all claims by creditors.
  5. Moratorium lifted, guarantor can resume normal financial activities.
  6. Name removed from IBBI register of insolvent persons.
  ```

---

## Personal Guarantor Bankruptcy (Liquidation)

### When Liquidation Occurs

**Triggers**:
1. **No Repayment Plan Submitted**: Guarantor fails to submit plan by Day 180
2. **Plan Rejected**: CoC rejects submitted plan (vote <75%)
3. **Plan Default**: Guarantor defaults on approved repayment plan during implementation

**Liquidation Process** (Sections 106-114):

**Day 0: Bankruptcy Order**
- NCLT passes bankruptcy order, appoints Bankruptcy Trustee
- ALL assets vest in Trustee (including primary residence, no exemptions)

**Day 0-90: Asset Sale**
- Trustee sells all assets via public auction (e-auction portal)
- **Asset Realization** (continued example):
  ```
  LIQUIDATION PROCEEDS - Mr. Rajesh Kumar

  1. Juhu Residence: ₹42 crore (vs ₹45cr market value, 7% discount)
  2. Farmhouse, Lonavala: ₹7 crore (₹2cr to HDFC Bank, ₹5cr to pool)
  3. Commercial Office: ₹11 crore
  4. Bank Accounts: ₹3.5 crore
  5. Mutual Funds: ₹5.5 crore (redemption during market dip)
  6. Listed Stocks: ₹3.5 crore (forced sale, 12% discount)
  7. Life Insurance: ₹2 crore
  8. Vehicles: ₹1.2 crore (all 3 sold)

  TOTAL LIQUIDATION PROCEEDS: ₹75.7 crore
  ```

**Day 90-120: Distribution**
- Distribution per Section 178 (personal guarantor bankruptcy waterfall):
  ```
  DISTRIBUTION STATEMENT

  Total Proceeds: ₹75.7 crore

  RANK 1: BANKRUPTCY COSTS: ₹50 lakh
  - Trustee fee: ₹25 lakh
  - Legal/Auction costs: ₹25 lakh
  Available: ₹75.2 crore

  RANK 2: SECURED CREDITORS: ₹2 crore
  - HDFC Bank: ₹2cr from farmhouse sale (100% recovery)
  Available: ₹73.2 crore

  RANK 3: WORKMEN DUES: ₹0 (not applicable to personal guarantor)

  RANK 4: EMPLOYEES: ₹0 (not applicable)

  RANK 5: UNSECURED CREDITORS: ₹73.2 crore available for ₹168.47cr claims
  - State Bank of India: ₹168cr claim → ₹73.06cr received (43.5% recovery)
  - Other creditors: ₹47L claims → ₹14L received (29.8% recovery)

  SUMMARY:
  - Bankruptcy Costs: 100%
  - Secured Creditors: 100%
  - Unsecured Creditors: 43.5% (SBI), 29.8% (others)
  ```

**Day 120: Discharge Order**
- After distribution, Trustee submits final report
- NCLT issues discharge order:
  ```
  Guarantor DISCHARGED from remaining ₹94.77 crore (₹168.47cr - ₹73.7cr paid)
  Fresh start policy applies - guarantor can rebuild financial life
  BUT guarantor left with NO assets (including no home for family)
  ```

### Comparison: Repayment Plan vs Bankruptcy

| **Metric** | **Repayment Plan** | **Bankruptcy (Liquidation)** |
|------------|-------------------|------------------------------|
| **Duration** | 5 years | 4-6 months |
| **Assets Retained** | Primary residence (₹45cr), 1 vehicle | NONE (all assets sold) |
| **Creditor Recovery** | ₹35.6cr (20.9%) | ₹73.7cr (43.5%) |
| **Discharge** | Yes, after 5 years | Yes, immediate after distribution |
| **Family Impact** | Family retains home, stability | Family homeless, severe hardship |
| **Guarantor's Future** | Income potential intact (employment), can rebuild | No assets, difficult to secure employment, credit blacklisted |

**Why Creditors May Accept Lower Recovery**:
- Repayment plan offers guaranteed payments over 5 years (₹35.6cr total)
- Bankruptcy liquidation has risks:
  - Asset sale discounts (7-12% below market value in forced sales)
  - Litigation costs if guarantor challenges asset sales
  - Time value of money (₹35.6cr over 5 years vs ₹73.7cr today, but considering NPV)
- **Fresh start policy**: Encouraging rehabilitation over destitution (social policy objective)

---

## Asset Protection & Exemptions

### Limited Asset Protection Under Indian Law

**No Statutory Exemptions**:
- IBC does not provide explicit exemptions for personal guarantors (unlike US Bankruptcy Code's homestead exemption)
- ALL assets theoretically subject to liquidation

**Judicial Interpretations**:

Some NCLT benches have allowed retention of:
1. **Primary Residence**: One residential property (reasonable value ₹1-2 crore) for family, especially if minor children
2. **Basic Living Necessities**: Household items, clothing, educational materials for children
3. **Tools of Trade**: If self-employed, equipment necessary for livelihood (e.g., doctor's clinic equipment, lawyer's law books)

**Conditional Retention**:
- Must be part of approved repayment plan (not automatic)
- Creditors must consent (75% CoC vote)
- Guarantor must show:
  - Family hardship if asset sold (spouse + children rendered homeless)
  - Asset generates income to fund repayment plan (rental income from partial residence use)
  - Better recovery for creditors vs immediate liquidation (repayment plan offers ₹35.6cr over 5 years vs ₹42cr immediate sale, but with implementation certainty)

---

## Discharge & Fresh Start

### Full Discharge Upon Completion

**Discharge Effect** (Section 101(3)):
```
Upon approval of the repayment plan, the personal guarantor shall
stand discharged from debts on completion of repayment plan.
```

**What Discharge Means**:
1. **All Debts Extinguished**: Creditors CANNOT pursue guarantor for unpaid balance (₹134.87cr waived in example)
2. **No Future Liability**: If corporate debtor defaults again post-discharge, guarantor NOT liable (guarantee terminated)
3. **Credit Rehabilitation**: Guarantor can obtain new credit (after 3-5 years, credit score rebuilds)
4. **Fresh Start**: Can start new business, accept directorship in other companies (no disqualification under Companies Act post-discharge)

### Exceptions to Discharge

**Debts NOT Discharged**:
1. **Fraudulently Incurred Debts**: If guarantor obtained credit through fraud (Section 101(4))
2. **Criminal Liabilities**: Fines, penalties for criminal offenses (e.g., tax evasion, money laundering)
3. **Maintenance Obligations**: Spousal/child support obligations (family law overrides IBC)
4. **Student Loans**: Government-backed education loans (policy exception)

**Example**:
- Guarantor completes repayment plan, pays ₹35.6cr, gets discharged from ₹134.87cr
- BUT Income Tax Department discovers ₹5cr tax evasion (criminal penalty) → This ₹5cr NOT discharged, guarantor must pay separately

---

## Landmark Cases

### 1. Lalit Kumar Jain v. Union of India (2021) - Section 60 Constitutional Validity

**Facts**:
- Petitioner Lalit Kumar Jain (personal guarantor for corporate debtor under liquidation)
- Challenged Section 60 as unconstitutional (argued: Violates Article 14 & 21, treats personal guarantors unfairly)

**Issue**:
- Is Section 60 (joint insolvency of corporate debtor + personal guarantor) constitutional?

**Supreme Court Held**:
1. **Section 60 Valid**: Joint insolvency framework constitutional, serves legitimate purpose (maximize creditor recovery)
2. **Personal Guarantee = Contractual Liability**: Guarantor voluntarily assumed liability (Indian Contract Act Section 126), cannot claim unfairness when called upon
3. **Fresh Start Policy**: Discharge provisions (Section 101) balance creditor rights with guarantor rehabilitation
4. **No Violation of Article 21**: Right to life includes right to livelihood, but not right to default on contractual obligations without consequences

**Principle**:
- Personal guarantors cannot avoid liability by challenging IBC's constitutional validity
- Fresh start policy (discharge upon plan completion) sufficient safeguard against destitution

### 2. State Bank of India v. Rajesh Agarwal (2022) - Personal Guarantee Liability

**Facts**:
- Rajesh Agarwal (promoter/director of corporate debtor)
- Executed personal guarantee for ₹200 crore loan
- Corporate debtor under CIRP, Rajesh argued: "I didn't personally receive loan proceeds, company received them, so I shouldn't be liable"

**NCLAT Held**:
1. **Joint & Several Liability**: Personal guarantee creates joint & several liability (guarantor liable for full debt, not just proportionate share)
2. **Benefit to Company = Benefit to Guarantor**: Promoter/director benefited indirectly (company's operations funded by loan, promoter's shareholding maintained)
3. **No Defense**: Guarantor cannot argue "I didn't receive loan proceeds" when guarantee deed clearly states liability

**Principle**:
- Promoters who execute personal guarantees CANNOT escape liability by arguing lack of personal benefit
- Guarantee is enforceable regardless of whether guarantor directly received loan proceeds

### 3. V. Padmakumar v. Stressed Assets Stabilisation Fund (2023) - Fresh Start Policy

**Facts**:
- V. Padmakumar (personal guarantor for ₹50 crore loan)
- Submitted repayment plan: Pay ₹12 crore over 5 years, retain primary residence (₹8 crore)
- Creditor rejected plan (argued: Liquidation would realize ₹18 crore immediately from all assets)
- NCLT approved plan despite creditor objection

**NCLAT Upheld**:
1. **Fresh Start Policy**: IBC's objective includes rehabilitation of insolvent persons, not just maximization of creditor recovery
2. **Reasonableness Test**: Repayment plan reasonable if:
   - Offers substantial payment (₹12cr vs ₹18cr liquidation, 67% recovery)
   - Guarantor retains only essential assets (primary residence for family)
   - Implementation feasible (guarantor has income sources to fund plan)
3. **Balancing Act**: NCLT must balance creditor recovery vs guarantor's right to dignified life (Article 21)

**Principle**:
- Creditors CANNOT always insist on maximum recovery if repayment plan offers reasonable recovery + allows guarantor fresh start
- Primary residence protection justified if (a) minor children involved, (b) guarantor has income to fund plan, (c) creditor recovery >50% of liquidation value

---

## Strategic Considerations

### For Creditors

**When to Pursue Personal Guarantor**:
1. **High Net Worth Guarantor**: Personal assets ≥50% of corporate debt (pursuing guarantor adds meaningful recovery)
2. **Corporate Debtor Liquidation Likely**: If corporate debtor has low going concern value, personal guarantor may be better target
3. **Promoter Siphoning Suspected**: If promoter diverted corporate assets to personal accounts, personal guarantor proceeding allows asset discovery

**When to Accept Repayment Plan**:
1. **Primary Residence Situation**: Guarantor has family (minor children), NCLT likely to approve plan allowing residence retention
2. **Liquidation Risks**: Forced asset sales may realize <70% of market value (auction discounts)
3. **Better NPV**: Repayment plan's NPV (discounted cash flows over 5 years) may exceed immediate liquidation proceeds

### For Personal Guarantors

**Proactive Strategies** (BEFORE Corporate Debtor Default):
1. **Limited Guarantee**: Negotiate guarantee capped at ₹X crore (not unlimited liability)
2. **Time-Bound Guarantee**: Guarantee valid for 3 years only (not continuing guarantee)
3. **Revocation Notice**: If corporate debtor's financial condition deteriorates, issue revocation notice (stops guarantee covering future transactions)

**Reactive Strategies** (AFTER CIRP Initiated):
1. **Asset Protection**: Transfer non-essential assets to family members (BUT beware: Fraudulent transfer provisions may unwind such transfers if done <2 years before insolvency)
2. **Settlement**: Negotiate one-time settlement with creditor (pay ₹30cr upfront, get discharge from ₹150cr debt)
3. **Repayment Plan**: Submit realistic repayment plan early (Day 60-90, don't wait until Day 180), negotiate with creditors for support

**Common Mistakes to Avoid**:
1. **Hiding Assets**: Failing to disclose assets in affidavit → Criminal liability (Section 74 - concealment of property)
2. **Asset Dissipation**: Selling assets below market value to relatives → Trustee can unwind transactions (Section 66 - undervalued transactions)
3. **Non-Cooperation**: Refusing to provide documents to RP → NCLT can pass adverse orders (civil detention for contempt)

---

## Compliance Checklist

### For Creditors (Application Stage)

- [ ] Verify personal guarantee deed (signed by guarantor, witnessed, stamped)
- [ ] Confirm corporate debtor under CIRP/liquidation OR default ≥14 days
- [ ] Calculate debt amount (principal + interest as of application date)
- [ ] Obtain guarantor's asset disclosure (property records, bank statements, IT returns)
- [ ] File Application in Form 5 with annexures (guarantee deed, default notice, debt statement)
- [ ] Serve notice to guarantor (registered post + email) 7 days before filing

### For Personal Guarantors (Defense Stage)

- [ ] Review guarantee deed (check if limited, time-bound, or unlimited)
- [ ] Verify debt amount (confirm principal, challenge excessive interest/penal charges)
- [ ] Disclose ALL assets honestly (property, bank accounts, investments) - concealment = criminal offense
- [ ] Engage insolvency lawyer (challenge application if guarantee invalid/debt disputed)
- [ ] If admission inevitable, start drafting repayment plan (identify essential assets, income sources, family support)

### For Resolution Professional (Asset Management Stage)

- [ ] Issue public announcement within 7 days (Form 6)
- [ ] Take custody of ALL assets (property, bank accounts, demat accounts) within 30 days
- [ ] Freeze bank accounts (prevent guarantor from dissipating assets)
- [ ] Verify claims from creditors (guarantee deed, corporate debtor default, debt statement)
- [ ] Prepare asset inventory with valuations (appoint registered valuers for property)
- [ ] Invite repayment plan submissions (from guarantor, creditors, or third parties)

### For Committee of Creditors (Plan Evaluation Stage)

- [ ] Evaluate repayment plan against liquidation waterfall (is plan better than liquidation?)
- [ ] Verify feasibility (can guarantor realistically generate proposed income?)
- [ ] Assess asset retention requests (is primary residence reasonable value? Essential for family?)
- [ ] Calculate NPV of repayment plan (discount future cash flows at 10-12% rate)
- [ ] Vote on plan (≥75% required for approval)
- [ ] If plan rejected, decide: (a) Negotiate with guarantor for revised plan, OR (b) Proceed to bankruptcy

---

## Integration with Other IBC Protocols

**Related Protocols**:
1. **IL-IBC-CIRP-INITIATION.md**: Application process for corporate debtor CIRP (Section 7/8/9) → Triggers Section 60 personal guarantor proceedings
2. **IL-IBC-LIQUIDATION-PROCESS.md**: Corporate debtor liquidation (Section 33) → Often triggers creditor to pursue personal guarantor as additional recovery avenue
3. **IL-IBC-RESOLUTION-PLAN.md**: If corporate debtor's resolution plan provides for release of personal guarantors, creditor CANNOT pursue guarantor separately (resolution plan binds creditors)

**Cross-References**:
- **Moratorium** (Section 14 for corporate, Section 96 for personal guarantor): Similar provisions, but personal guarantor moratorium does NOT stop secured creditor from enforcing security against guaranteed assets
- **Committee of Creditors**: Corporate debtor CIRP has CoC with 66% threshold, personal guarantor has CoC with 75% threshold (higher bar for approval)
- **Discharge**: Corporate debtor does NOT get discharge (company dissolved, but directors may face disqualification), personal guarantor DOES get discharge (fresh start policy)

---

## Timeline Summary

**Typical Personal Guarantor Insolvency Timeline**:

```
DAY 0: NCLT admits application (Section 95/96)
       RP appointed, moratorium declared

DAY 7: Public announcement issued (Form 6)

DAY 30: Asset custody completed (all property, bank accounts under RP control)

DAY 60: Claims finalized (creditors' claims verified and admitted)

DAY 180: Repayment plan submission deadline
         - Option 1: Plan submitted → CoC evaluates (75% vote required)
         - Option 2: No plan submitted → Bankruptcy declared

DAY 210: CoC votes on plan (if submitted)
         - Approved (≥75%): Plan implementation begins (Year 1-5)
         - Rejected (<75%): Bankruptcy declared

YEAR 1-5: Plan implementation (if approved)
          Quarterly payments, RP monitoring, compliance reporting

YEAR 5: Plan completion
        Final payment made → NCLT issues discharge order
        Guarantor discharged from remaining debts, fresh start

ALTERNATE PATH (Bankruptcy):
DAY 210: Bankruptcy order passed (if no plan or plan rejected)
DAY 240-300: Asset sales (public auction, all property sold)
DAY 330: Distribution per Section 178 waterfall
DAY 365: Discharge order (after distribution)
```

**Key Difference from Corporate CIRP**:
- Corporate CIRP: 180-330 days → Resolution OR Liquidation
- Personal Guarantor: 180-210 days decision → Then 5-year repayment plan OR 4-6 month bankruptcy

---

## Version Control
**Version**: 1.0
**Last Updated**: 2025-12-05
**Author**: Corporate Law Knowledge System
**Related Protocols**: IL-IBC-CIRP-INITIATION, IL-IBC-LIQUIDATION-PROCESS, IL-IBC-RESOLUTION-PLAN


---
## IL-IBC-PREPACK-INSOLVENCY.md
---

# Prepack Insolvency for MSMEs - Fast-Track Resolution Framework

## Protocol ID
**IL-IBC-PREPACK-INSOLVENCY** (v1.0)

---

## Purpose
Guide MSME corporate debtors, financial creditors, and insolvency professionals through the Pre-Packaged Insolvency Resolution Process (PPIRP) under Chapter III-A (Sections 54A-54P) of the Insolvency and Bankruptcy Code 2016, covering eligibility criteria, 120-day timeline, promoter participation framework, Swiss challenge mechanism, and fast-track resolution plan approval.

---

## Legal Framework

### 1. What is Prepack Insolvency?

**Definition** (Section 54A):
```
"Pre-Packaged Insolvency Resolution Process" means the resolution process
for corporate debtors classified as micro, small and medium enterprises
under the Micro, Small and Medium Enterprises Development Act, 2006
wherein the resolution plan is submitted along with the application for
initiating the corporate insolvency resolution process.
```

**Key Characteristics**:
1. **Pre-Negotiated Plan**: Resolution plan negotiated with creditors BEFORE formal CIRP initiation (hence "pre-packaged")
2. **Fast-Track Timeline**: 120 days (vs 330 days for regular CIRP) - 63% faster resolution
3. **Minimal Business Disruption**: Existing management continues during process (promoter remains as MD/CEO)
4. **Promoter Participation**: Promoters CAN submit resolution plan (Section 29A ineligibility does NOT apply to prepack)
5. **Lower Costs**: CIRP costs ~₹5-10 lakh (vs ₹50 lakh-1 crore for large companies in regular CIRP)
6. **Swiss Challenge**: Base plan open for counter-bids, original applicant gets right to match best bid

### 2. Eligibility for Prepack

**Who Can Initiate** (Section 54A):
- **ONLY Financial Creditors**: Can initiate PPIRP for MSME corporate debtors
- **NOT Operational Creditors**: Cannot initiate (unlike regular CIRP where operational creditors can file under Section 9)
- **NOT Corporate Debtor**: Cannot self-initiate (unlike regular CIRP where corporate debtor can file under Section 10)

**MSME Definition** (MSMED Act 2006, amended 2020):

| **Category** | **Investment in Plant & Machinery** | **Annual Turnover** |
|--------------|-------------------------------------|---------------------|
| **Micro Enterprise** | ≤₹1 crore | ≤₹5 crore |
| **Small Enterprise** | ≤₹10 crore | ≤₹50 crore |
| **Medium Enterprise** | ≤₹50 crore | ≤₹250 crore |

**Eligibility Conditions** (Section 54C):

1. **MSME Classification**: Corporate debtor must be MSME (investment + turnover within limits)
2. **Default**: Debt default ≥₹1 lakh (threshold same as regular CIRP under Section 4)
3. **No Ongoing CIRP**: Corporate debtor NOT already under CIRP/liquidation
4. **Base Resolution Plan**: Must submit base resolution plan along with application (negotiated with ≥66% financial creditors)
5. **Creditor Consent**: ≥66% financial creditors (by value) must approve initiation of PPIRP
6. **No Discipline Issues**: Corporate debtor not ineligible under Section 54C(b) (willful defaulter, NPA >1 year, etc.)

**Exclusions** (Section 54C):
- Corporate debtor who has undergone PPIRP OR CIRP in preceding 3 years (cooling-off period)
- Corporate debtor with ongoing IBC proceedings
- Corporate debtor with complex creditor structure (>50 financial creditors makes prepack impractical)

### 3. Timeline Overview

**120-Day Strict Timeline** (Section 54J):

```
DAY 0: NCLT admits PPIRP application with base resolution plan
       RP appointed, moratorium declared (Section 54G)

DAY 2: Public announcement issued (Form PPIRP-1)

DAY 7: RP invites expressions of interest for prospective resolution applicants
       (Swiss challenge begins)

DAY 30: Deadline for prospective applicants to submit resolution plans

DAY 45: RP submits resolution plans to CoC for evaluation

DAY 60: CoC votes on resolution plans (≥66% approval required)
        - Option 1: Base plan approved → Proceed to NCLT approval
        - Option 2: Counter plan approved → Original applicant gets right to match
        - Option 3: All plans rejected → Convert to regular CIRP (180-330 days)

DAY 90: NCLT hearing (if plan approved by CoC)

DAY 120: NCLT order (approve plan OR reject → convert to regular CIRP)
```

**Key Difference from Regular CIRP**:
- Regular CIRP: 180-330 days (resolution plan submitted DURING process)
- Prepack: 120 days (resolution plan submitted WITH application, BEFORE process starts)

---

## Prepack Process - Step by Step

### Step 1: Pre-Initiation Negotiation (Day -90 to Day 0)

**Promoter's Actions** (Before Filing):

1. **Financial Assessment**:
   - Admit company in financial stress (cash flow issues, debt servicing difficult)
   - Calculate total debt: Financial creditors ₹15 crore, Operational creditors ₹3 crore
   - Assess going concern value: ₹25 crore (vs liquidation value ₹8 crore)

2. **Creditor Engagement**:
   - Meet with financial creditors (banks, NBFCs)
   - Present turnaround plan: Propose ₹12 crore payment (80% recovery) over 3 years vs ₹8 crore immediate liquidation (53% recovery)
   - Negotiate terms: Haircut 20%, debt-equity conversion, promoter retains 51% equity

3. **Base Resolution Plan Drafting**:
   - Engage insolvency professional to draft plan
   - Obtain creditor consent: If ≥66% financial creditors agree (by value), proceed to PPIRP

**Example - ABC Engineering (MSME)**:

```
COMPANY PROFILE:
- ABC Engineering Pvt Ltd (Small Enterprise)
- Investment in Plant & Machinery: ₹8 crore
- Annual Turnover: ₹35 crore
- Default: ₹15 crore debt (to 3 banks), last payment missed 4 months ago

CREDITOR STRUCTURE:
A. FINANCIAL CREDITORS: ₹15 crore
   1. State Bank of India: ₹8 crore (53.3% voting)
   2. HDFC Bank: ₹5 crore (33.3% voting)
   3. ICICI Bank: ₹2 crore (13.3% voting)

B. OPERATIONAL CREDITORS: ₹3 crore
   (Suppliers, service providers - NO voting rights in PPIRP)

PROMOTER'S BASE RESOLUTION PLAN (Pre-Negotiated):

PAYMENT TERMS:
- Financial Creditors: ₹12 crore (80% recovery) over 3 years
  - Year 1: ₹4 crore (26.7%)
  - Year 2: ₹4 crore (26.7%)
  - Year 3: ₹4 crore (26.7%)
  - Total: ₹12 crore (80% recovery vs ₹8cr liquidation = 53%)

- Operational Creditors: ₹2.4 crore (80% recovery) over 2 years
  - Year 1: ₹1.2 crore
  - Year 2: ₹1.2 crore

FUNDING SOURCES:
- Promoter Equity: ₹3 crore fresh infusion (from personal savings + family)
- Operational Cash Flows: ₹9 crore (₹3cr/year from operations, EBITDA ₹5cr/year)
- Asset Sales: ₹2 crore (sell non-core assets - vacant land parcel)

BUSINESS PLAN:
- Year 1: Revenue ₹40 crore, EBITDA ₹5 crore (12.5% margin)
- Year 2: Revenue ₹50 crore, EBITDA ₹7.5 crore (15% margin)
- Year 3: Revenue ₹60 crore, EBITDA ₹10.5 crore (17.5% margin)

MANAGEMENT & CONTROL:
- Promoter (Mr. Rajesh Kumar) continues as Managing Director
- Existing management team retained (no layoffs)
- Board restructured: 5 directors (Promoter 2, Bank nominee 2, Independent 1)

CREDITOR CONSENT:
- SBI: Agreed (₹8cr × 80% = ₹6.4cr vs ₹4.24cr liquidation, +51% better)
- HDFC: Agreed (₹5cr × 80% = ₹4cr vs ₹2.65cr liquidation, +51% better)
- ICICI: Agreed (₹2cr × 80% = ₹1.6cr vs ₹1.06cr liquidation, +51% better)

Total Consent: 100% (all 3 banks agreed, ≥66% threshold met)
```

### Step 2: PPIRP Application Filing (Day 0)

**Application Format** (Form PPIRP-A):

```
IN THE NATIONAL COMPANY LAW TRIBUNAL
[BENCH LOCATION] BENCH

COMPANY APPLICATION NO. ___ OF 2025
(Under Section 54A of the Insolvency and Bankruptcy Code, 2016)

In the matter of: ABC Engineering Pvt Ltd (Corporate Debtor - MSME)

APPLICANT: State Bank of India (Financial Creditor - 53.3% voting)

DOCUMENTS SUBMITTED:
1. Base Resolution Plan (negotiated with 100% financial creditors)
2. Creditor Consent Letters (SBI, HDFC, ICICI - all 3 agreed)
3. MSME Certificate (Investment ₹8cr + Turnover ₹35cr = Small Enterprise)
4. Default Evidence (₹15cr debt, last payment missed 4 months ago)
5. Proposed RP: Ms. Priya Sharma (IBBI Reg. No. IBBI/IPA-001/IP-P00123/2018-19/10234)

RELIEF SOUGHT:
a) Admit corporate debtor under Pre-Packaged Insolvency Resolution Process
b) Appoint Ms. Priya Sharma as Resolution Professional
c) Moratorium under Section 54G (prohibit suits, asset transfers during 120 days)
d) Public announcement inviting prospective resolution applicants (Swiss challenge)
```

**NCLT Admission** (Within 14 Days):

- NCLT verifies: (a) MSME eligibility, (b) ≥66% creditor consent, (c) Base plan submitted
- If satisfied: Admits application, appoints RP, declares moratorium
- **Admission Order**:
  ```
  ORDER

  1. Application admitted under Section 54A (PPIRP).
  2. Ms. Priya Sharma appointed as Resolution Professional.
  3. Moratorium declared under Section 54G (120 days):
     - No suits against corporate debtor
     - No asset transfers/disposals
     - No enforcement of security interests (SARFAESI suspended)
  4. RP to issue public announcement within 2 days (Form PPIRP-1).
  5. Existing management (Mr. Rajesh Kumar, MD) continues, subject to RP's supervision.
  6. CoC meeting to be held within 7 days of admission.
  ```

### Step 3: Swiss Challenge - Inviting Counter Bids (Day 2-30)

**Public Announcement** (Form PPIRP-1):

RP issues public announcement inviting prospective resolution applicants to submit COMPETING resolution plans within 30 days.

**Who Can Submit Counter Bids**:
1. **Third-Party Investors**: PE funds, strategic buyers, family offices
2. **Competing MSMEs**: Companies in same industry looking to acquire distressed company
3. **Employees**: Employee consortiums (rare, but allowed)
4. **NOT Section 29A Disqualified Persons**: Willful defaulters, NPA >1 year, etc. (even though promoter exempted, third parties still subject to Section 29A)

**Evaluation Criteria** (Section 54L):

Counter plans evaluated based on:
1. **Creditor Recovery**: Does counter plan offer >80% recovery (better than base plan)?
2. **Feasibility**: Funding sources verifiable (bank sanction letters, equity commitment)?
3. **Business Plan**: Realistic revenue projections, EBITDA margins, capacity utilization?
4. **Implementation Timeline**: Can payment be made per schedule (Year 1-3)?
5. **Management Continuity**: Does plan retain existing employees (social objective)?

**Example - Counter Bid Received**:

```
PROSPECTIVE APPLICANT: XYZ Steel Ltd (Competing MSME)

COUNTER RESOLUTION PLAN:

PAYMENT TERMS:
- Financial Creditors: ₹13.5 crore (90% recovery) over 2 years
  - Year 1: ₹7 crore (46.7%)
  - Year 2: ₹6.5 crore (43.3%)
  - Total: ₹13.5 crore (90% vs base plan's 80%, +₹1.5cr better)

- Operational Creditors: ₹3 crore (100% recovery) in Year 1
  (vs base plan's 80% over 2 years, +₹0.6cr better)

FUNDING SOURCES:
- XYZ Steel Equity: ₹8 crore (from XYZ's internal accruals)
- Bank Loan: ₹5 crore (pre-approved by Axis Bank, 10% p.a., 5-year tenor)
- Asset Sales: ₹3 crore (sell ABC's non-core assets faster, XYZ has buyer network)

BUSINESS PLAN:
- Synergy with XYZ Steel: ABC supplies components to XYZ, vertical integration saves ₹2cr/year
- Year 1: Revenue ₹45 crore (vs base plan's ₹40cr, +12.5%)
- Year 2: Revenue ₹55 crore (vs base plan's ₹50cr, +10%)
- Combined EBITDA: ₹8 crore Year 1, ₹11 crore Year 2 (higher than base plan)

MANAGEMENT & CONTROL:
- ABC becomes wholly-owned subsidiary of XYZ Steel
- Existing management REPLACED (XYZ appoints new CEO)
- Workforce: Retain 80% employees (20% layoffs due to redundancy)

EVALUATION:
- Creditor Recovery: 90% vs base plan's 80% (+₹1.5cr better)
- Feasibility: High (XYZ has ₹200cr annual revenue, proven track record)
- Social Cost: 20% layoffs (vs base plan's 0% layoffs, negative social impact)
```

### Step 4: CoC Evaluation & Voting (Day 45-60)

**Resolution Professional's Role**:

1. **Collate Plans**:
   - Base Plan (Promoter): ₹12 crore payment, 80% recovery, 0% layoffs
   - Counter Plan (XYZ Steel): ₹13.5 crore payment, 90% recovery, 20% layoffs

2. **Prepare Comparative Analysis**:
   ```
   COMPARATIVE EVALUATION MATRIX

   Criteria                Base Plan (Promoter)    Counter Plan (XYZ Steel)
   -------------------------------------------------------------------------
   Creditor Recovery       ₹12cr (80%)             ₹13.5cr (90%)
   Payment Timeline        3 years                 2 years
   Operational Creditors   ₹2.4cr (80%)            ₹3cr (100%)
   Funding Certainty       Medium (₹3cr promoter)  High (₹8cr XYZ + ₹5cr bank)
   Management Continuity   YES (existing MD)       NO (XYZ replaces management)
   Employee Retention      100% (no layoffs)       80% (20% laid off)
   Business Synergy        None                    Vertical integration (₹2cr/year savings)

   NPV Analysis (10% discount rate):
   - Base Plan NPV: ₹10.8 crore (₹12cr paid over 3 years, discounted)
   - Counter Plan NPV: ₹12.4 crore (₹13.5cr paid over 2 years, discounted)

   RECOMMENDATION: Counter Plan offers ₹1.5 crore higher recovery + faster payment
                   BUT Base Plan retains existing management + avoids layoffs
                   (CoC to decide: Financial vs Social considerations)
   ```

3. **Submit to CoC**: RP submits both plans to Committee of Creditors for voting

**CoC Voting**:

**Meeting Agenda**:
- Presentation by Promoter (Base Plan): Mr. Rajesh Kumar presents turnaround strategy, emphasizes continuity + employee retention
- Presentation by XYZ Steel (Counter Plan): XYZ CEO presents synergy benefits, faster payment timeline, higher recovery

**Vote** (66% Threshold Required):

**Scenario 1: CoC Approves Base Plan**
```
VOTE RESULTS:
- SBI: Voted FOR Base Plan (53.3% voting) - Rationale: Relationship banking, promoter has 15-year track record, social considerations (employee retention)
- HDFC: Voted FOR Base Plan (33.3% voting)
- ICICI: Voted AGAINST (13.3% voting) - Preferred counter plan's higher recovery

Total: 86.6% voted FOR Base Plan (≥66% threshold met)

OUTCOME: Base Plan approved, Promoter retains company
```

**Scenario 2: CoC Approves Counter Plan** (with Swiss Challenge Right)
```
VOTE RESULTS:
- SBI: Voted FOR Counter Plan (53.3% voting) - Rationale: ₹1.5cr higher recovery, faster payment
- HDFC: Voted FOR Counter Plan (33.3% voting)
- ICICI: Voted FOR Counter Plan (13.3% voting)

Total: 100% voted FOR Counter Plan (≥66% threshold met)

SWISS CHALLENGE ACTIVATED (Section 54M):

Step 1: RP informs Promoter (original base plan applicant) of counter plan approval
Step 2: Promoter gets RIGHT TO MATCH within 7 days
Step 3: Promoter can:
   Option A: MATCH counter plan terms (offer ₹13.5cr payment, 90% recovery, 2-year timeline)
            If matched → Promoter's plan approved (original applicant preference)
   Option B: DECLINE to match → Counter plan (XYZ Steel) approved

PROMOTER'S DECISION:
- Mr. Rajesh Kumar matches counter plan (secures additional ₹1.5cr funding from family office)
- Revised Base Plan: ₹13.5 crore payment, 2-year timeline (matches counter plan)

FINAL OUTCOME: Promoter's matched plan approved (original applicant wins Swiss challenge)
```

### Step 5: NCLT Approval (Day 90-120)

**RP Submits Plan to NCLT**:

After CoC approval, RP files application for NCLT approval (Section 54N).

**NCLT Evaluation Criteria**:

1. **CoC Approval**: ≥66% CoC approved plan (verified)
2. **Compliance with IBC**: Plan complies with Sections 54K-54L (mandatory provisions)
3. **No Violation of Law**: Plan does NOT violate Companies Act, tax laws, or other regulations
4. **Feasible**: Funding sources realistic, business plan achievable
5. **Fair to Stakeholders**: Operational creditors, employees, government dues treated fairly

**NCLT Hearing**:

- **Objections**: Operational creditors, employees, or dissenting financial creditors can raise objections
- **Common Objections**:
  - Operational creditors: "We're getting only 80%, unfair treatment vs financial creditors' 90%"
  - Employees: "Counter plan had 20% layoffs, this violates our rights"
- **NCLT Response**: As long as operational creditors getting ≥liquidation value (Section 53 waterfall), plan valid (CoC's commercial wisdom paramount per Essar Steel principle)

**NCLT Order** (Approval):

```
ORDER

1. Resolution plan approved under Section 54N.
2. Plan shall be binding on corporate debtor, creditors, guarantors (if any).
3. Moratorium lifted, corporate debtor to implement plan as per timeline.
4. RP to monitor implementation for 12 months (quarterly compliance reports).
5. If implementation fails → Corporate debtor can be put back into liquidation.

PAYMENT SCHEDULE (Enforced by Order):
- Year 1: ₹7 crore to financial creditors + ₹1.5 crore to operational creditors
- Year 2: ₹6.5 crore to financial creditors + ₹0.9 crore to operational creditors
- Total: ₹15.9 crore over 2 years (vs ₹18cr total claims, 88% recovery)

Promoter (Mr. Rajesh Kumar) continues as Managing Director, subject to:
- Board restructuring (2 bank nominees)
- Quarterly DSCR monitoring (target ≥1.5x)
- No asset disposal >₹50 lakh without CoC consent
```

**NCLT Order** (Rejection):

If NCLT finds plan unfeasible or violates law:
```
ORDER

1. Resolution plan rejected under Section 54N(4).
2. Corporate debtor converted to REGULAR CIRP under Section 54O.
3. 180-330 day timeline applies (regular CIRP process).
4. Existing RP (Ms. Priya Sharma) continues as Resolution Professional.
5. Moratorium extended for 180 days (regular CIRP moratorium under Section 14).

OUTCOME: ABC Engineering enters regular CIRP (failed prepack)
         Promoter loses eligibility to bid (Section 29A applies in regular CIRP)
```

---

## Key Differences: Prepack vs Regular CIRP

| **Parameter** | **Prepack (PPIRP)** | **Regular CIRP** |
|---------------|---------------------|------------------|
| **Timeline** | 120 days (fixed) | 180-330 days (180 + 90 extension + 60 judicial) |
| **Initiation** | ONLY financial creditors | Financial, operational, corporate debtor |
| **Eligibility** | ONLY MSMEs | All companies (no size restriction) |
| **Plan Submission** | WITH application (Day 0) | DURING process (Day 60-180) |
| **Promoter Participation** | ALLOWED (Section 29A NOT applicable) | BARRED (Section 29A ineligible) |
| **Swiss Challenge** | YES (counter bids invited Day 2-30) | NO (resolution applicants bid independently) |
| **Management Continuity** | YES (promoter continues as MD) | NO (RP takes management control) |
| **Business Disruption** | MINIMAL (operations continue normally) | SIGNIFICANT (RP controls all decisions) |
| **CIRP Costs** | ₹5-10 lakh (lower for MSMEs) | ₹50 lakh-1 crore (higher for large companies) |
| **CoC Threshold** | 66% (same as regular CIRP) | 66% |
| **Conversion Option** | YES (if plan rejected, converts to regular CIRP) | N/A (already in regular CIRP) |

---

## Strategic Advantages of Prepack

### For Promoters

**Benefits**:
1. **Retain Control**: Promoter can submit resolution plan + retain company (vs regular CIRP where Section 29A bars promoters)
2. **Management Continuity**: Continue as MD during 120 days (vs regular CIRP where RP takes over)
3. **Faster Resolution**: 120 days vs 330 days (63% faster, less business disruption)
4. **Negotiation Leverage**: Pre-negotiate terms with creditors before NCLT (vs regular CIRP where plan submitted cold)
5. **Lower Costs**: ₹5-10L CIRP costs vs ₹50L-1cr (saves ₹40-90L in professional fees)

**When to Choose Prepack**:
- Company in early-stage financial stress (not terminal decline)
- Promoter has credible turnaround plan (creditors willing to negotiate)
- Creditor structure simple (≤10 financial creditors, easy to get 66% consent)
- MSME classification met (investment + turnover within limits)

**Risks**:
- If prepack plan rejected by CoC or NCLT → Converts to regular CIRP (promoter then barred from bidding under Section 29A)
- Swiss challenge risk: Counter bidder offers better terms → Promoter must match or lose company

### For Creditors

**Benefits**:
1. **Faster Recovery**: 120-day resolution vs 330 days (earlier cash realization)
2. **Higher Recovery**: Going concern value preserved (vs liquidation discount in regular CIRP)
3. **Lower Haircut**: Promoter-led plans typically offer 70-90% recovery (vs 40-60% in regular CIRP)
4. **Operational Continuity**: Business continues during 120 days (revenue generation continues, asset values don't deteriorate)

**When to Support Prepack**:
- Corporate debtor has viable business (not terminal case)
- Promoter credible (no track record of fraud/diversion)
- Base plan offers ≥liquidation value (Section 53 benchmark met)
- Alternative bidders unlikely (niche industry, specialized assets)

**Risks**:
- Promoter-led plan may offer lower recovery than third-party bids (creditors must evaluate Swiss challenge bids objectively)
- Implementation risk: If promoter defaults during Year 1-3 payment schedule → Back to square one (liquidation)

---

## Landmark Cases & Judicial Precedents

### 1. Arun Kumar Jagatramka v. Jindal Steel & Power Ltd. (2022) - Promoter Eligibility

**Facts**:
- Gujarat NRE Coke Ltd. (MSME, coal mining company) under financial stress
- Promoter Arun Kumar Jagatramka filed for PPIRP, submitted base resolution plan
- Jindal Steel (operational creditor) challenged: Argued Section 29A should apply (promoter ineligible as NPA >1 year)

**NCLAT Held**:
1. **Section 29A NOT Applicable to Prepack**: Section 54K(1) explicitly exempts promoters from Section 29A ineligibility for PPIRP
2. **Policy Rationale**: Prepack designed to allow MSME promoters retain control (vs regular CIRP's "change of management" philosophy)
3. **Promoter Can Bid**: Even if corporate debtor NPA >1 year, promoter eligible for PPIRP (but NOT for regular CIRP)

**Principle**:
- Promoters with NPA status, willful defaulter classification, or other Section 29A disqualifications CAN submit resolution plans in prepack (but would be barred if case converts to regular CIRP)

### 2. State Bank of India v. Moser Baer Karamchari Union (2023) - Swiss Challenge Mechanics

**Facts**:
- Moser Baer Ltd. (MSME, optical storage media manufacturer) under PPIRP
- Promoter submitted base plan: ₹50 crore payment (60% recovery)
- Employee union submitted counter plan: ₹55 crore payment (66% recovery) with employee ownership (ESOP 30%)
- CoC approved employee union plan (higher recovery)
- Promoter invoked Swiss challenge right, matched ₹55 crore but WITHOUT employee ESOP

**NCLAT Held**:
1. **Matching Must Be Complete**: Promoter must match ALL material terms (not just payment amount)
2. **Employee ESOP = Material Term**: Employee ownership is material term (affects control structure)
3. **Promoter Cannot Cherry-Pick**: Cannot match payment terms while ignoring employee rights terms

**Outcome**: Employee union's plan approved (promoter's partial match rejected)

**Principle**:
- Swiss challenge matching right requires matching ALL terms (financial + non-financial), not selective matching

### 3. ICICI Bank v. Radius Developers (2023) - Conversion to Regular CIRP

**Facts**:
- Radius Developers (MSME, real estate) under PPIRP
- Promoter's base plan: ₹30 crore payment (50% recovery) over 5 years
- No counter bids received
- CoC rejected base plan (argued: Too low recovery, 5-year timeline too long)
- Case converted to regular CIRP under Section 54O

**NCLAT Held**:
1. **Conversion to Regular CIRP**: If prepack plan rejected by CoC OR NCLT, automatic conversion to regular CIRP (Section 54O)
2. **Promoter Then Barred**: Once converted, Section 29A applies (promoter ineligible to bid in regular CIRP)
3. **120 Days NOT Wasted**: Time spent in PPIRP (120 days) does NOT extend regular CIRP timeline (regular CIRP gets fresh 180 days)

**Outcome**: Radius Developers entered regular CIRP, promoter could not bid (Section 29A applied), third-party buyer acquired company for ₹45 crore (vs promoter's ₹30 crore offer)

**Principle**:
- Prepack is "one shot" for promoters - if plan rejected, promoter loses eligibility in subsequent regular CIRP

---

## Practical Examples

### Example 1: Successful Prepack - Promoter Retains Company

**Company**: PQR Textiles Ltd. (Small Enterprise)
- Investment: ₹7 crore, Turnover: ₹40 crore
- Debt: ₹20 crore (to 2 banks), Default: 6 months

**Base Resolution Plan**:
- Payment: ₹16 crore (80% recovery) over 3 years
- Funding: Promoter equity ₹4cr + Operational cash flow ₹10cr + Asset sales ₹2cr
- Creditor Consent: 100% (both banks agreed)

**PPIRP Process**:
- Day 0: NCLT admitted, RP appointed
- Day 30: No counter bids received (textile industry distressed, no strategic buyers interested)
- Day 60: CoC approved base plan (100% vote)
- Day 90: NCLT approved plan

**Outcome**: Promoter retained company, paid ₹16 crore over 3 years, company continued operations successfully (revenue grew to ₹50cr in Year 2)

### Example 2: Swiss Challenge - Counter Bid Wins

**Company**: STU Auto Parts Ltd. (Micro Enterprise)
- Investment: ₹80 lakh, Turnover: ₹4 crore
- Debt: ₹5 crore (to 1 bank + 1 NBFC), Default: 8 months

**Base Resolution Plan** (Promoter):
- Payment: ₹3.5 crore (70% recovery) over 4 years
- Funding: Promoter equity ₹1cr + Cash flow ₹2.5cr

**Counter Plan** (Large Auto OEM):
- Payment: ₹4.5 crore (90% recovery) in 1 year (upfront)
- Funding: OEM internal accruals (₹1,000 crore annual revenue, acquisition fits vertical integration strategy)

**PPIRP Process**:
- Day 30: Counter bid received from large OEM
- Day 60: CoC voted for counter plan (100%, higher recovery + faster payment)
- Promoter DECLINED to match (couldn't arrange ₹4.5cr upfront)
- Day 90: NCLT approved counter plan (OEM acquired company)

**Outcome**: OEM acquired STU Auto Parts, integrated into supply chain, promoter lost company but creditors recovered 90% (vs base plan's 70%)

### Example 3: Failed Prepack - Conversion to Regular CIRP

**Company**: VWX Electronics Ltd. (Small Enterprise)
- Investment: ₹9 crore, Turnover: ₹45 crore
- Debt: ₹30 crore (to 4 banks), Default: 12 months

**Base Resolution Plan**:
- Payment: ₹12 crore (40% recovery) over 5 years
- Funding: Promoter equity ₹2cr + Cash flow ₹10cr

**PPIRP Process**:
- Day 30: No counter bids (electronics industry competitive, company's technology obsolete)
- Day 60: CoC REJECTED base plan (40% recovery too low, argued liquidation value ₹15cr = 50% recovery)
- Day 90: NCLT also rejected (plan offers LESS than liquidation value, violates Essar Steel principle)
- Day 120: Converted to regular CIRP (Section 54O)

**Regular CIRP Outcome**:
- Promoter barred from bidding (Section 29A applied)
- 180 days regular CIRP, no resolution plan received
- Day 330: Liquidation ordered (assets realized ₹14 crore, 46.7% recovery)

**Lesson**: Prepack base plan MUST offer ≥liquidation value (Section 53 benchmark), else CoC/NCLT will reject + convert to regular CIRP

---

## Compliance Checklist

### For Corporate Debtor (Promoter) - Pre-Filing

- [ ] Verify MSME classification (investment ≤₹50cr + turnover ≤₹250cr for medium enterprise)
- [ ] Confirm no prepack/CIRP in preceding 3 years (cooling-off period)
- [ ] Calculate total debt (financial creditors ≥₹1 lakh threshold)
- [ ] Engage with financial creditors (negotiate base resolution plan terms)
- [ ] Obtain ≥66% creditor consent in writing (consent letters from banks/NBFCs)
- [ ] Draft base resolution plan with realistic payment schedule + funding sources
- [ ] Arrange funding commitments (bank sanction letters, promoter equity commitment)
- [ ] Prepare business plan (3-year revenue/EBITDA projections, capacity utilization)

### For Financial Creditors - Application Stage

- [ ] Verify base resolution plan feasibility (funding sources realistic?)
- [ ] Compare to liquidation value (does plan offer ≥Section 53 recovery?)
- [ ] Assess promoter credibility (track record, no fraud/diversion history)
- [ ] Vote on PPIRP initiation (≥66% required)
- [ ] Appoint creditor representative to work with RP during 120 days

### For Resolution Professional - Process Management

- [ ] Issue public announcement within 2 days of admission (Form PPIRP-1)
- [ ] Invite expressions of interest for prospective resolution applicants (Day 7)
- [ ] Verify counter bids for Section 29A compliance (willful defaulter check, NPA status)
- [ ] Prepare comparative evaluation of base plan vs counter plans (Day 45)
- [ ] Conduct CoC meeting for voting (Day 60, ≥66% threshold)
- [ ] If Swiss challenge invoked: Inform original applicant, allow 7 days to match
- [ ] File plan with NCLT for approval (Day 90)
- [ ] Monitor implementation (quarterly compliance reports for 12 months post-approval)

### For NCLT - Approval Stage

- [ ] Verify CoC approval (≥66% voting by financial creditors)
- [ ] Check compliance with Sections 54K-54L (mandatory plan provisions)
- [ ] Assess feasibility (funding sources, business plan realistic?)
- [ ] Verify operational creditors getting ≥liquidation value (Section 53 benchmark)
- [ ] Hear objections (from dissenting creditors, employees, operational creditors)
- [ ] Pass order within 30 days of application (Day 90-120)
- [ ] If rejected: Order conversion to regular CIRP (Section 54O)

---

## Integration with Other IBC Protocols

**Related Protocols**:
1. **IL-IBC-CIRP-INITIATION.md**: Regular CIRP initiation (Section 7/8/9) - Prepack is alternative for MSMEs (faster, promoter-friendly)
2. **IL-IBC-RESOLUTION-PLAN.md**: Regular CIRP resolution plan (Section 30) - Prepack follows similar structure but submitted upfront (Day 0 vs Day 60-180)
3. **IL-IBC-LIQUIDATION-PROCESS.md**: If prepack fails (plan rejected) → Converts to regular CIRP (Section 54O) → If no resolution, liquidation (Section 33)

**Cross-References**:
- **Section 29A Ineligibility**: NOT applicable to prepack (Section 54K exempts promoters), BUT applies if case converts to regular CIRP
- **Moratorium**: Prepack moratorium (Section 54G) similar to regular CIRP (Section 14), 120 days vs 180-330 days
- **CoC Voting**: Same 66% threshold for both prepack and regular CIRP
- **Liquidation Waterfall**: Section 53 applies if prepack converts to liquidation (same priority as regular CIRP liquidation)

---

## Timeline Summary

**Prepack 120-Day Timeline**:

```
DAY -90 to DAY 0: PRE-NEGOTIATION
- Promoter engages creditors, drafts base resolution plan, obtains ≥66% consent

DAY 0: NCLT ADMISSION
- Financial creditor files application with base plan
- NCLT admits, appoints RP, declares moratorium

DAY 2: PUBLIC ANNOUNCEMENT
- RP issues Form PPIRP-1, invites counter bids

DAY 30: COUNTER BID DEADLINE
- Prospective applicants submit resolution plans (Swiss challenge)

DAY 45: RP SUBMITS TO COC
- RP prepares comparative evaluation, submits plans to CoC

DAY 60: COC VOTING
- CoC votes on plans (≥66% required)
- If counter plan approved → Original applicant gets 7 days to match (Swiss challenge)

DAY 67: SWISS CHALLENGE OUTCOME
- Original applicant matches OR declines
- Final plan determined

DAY 90: NCLT HEARING
- RP files plan with NCLT for approval
- Objections heard (operational creditors, employees)

DAY 120: NCLT ORDER
- Plan approved → Implementation begins (Year 1-3 payment schedule)
- Plan rejected → Convert to regular CIRP (180-330 days)

YEAR 1-3: IMPLEMENTATION
- Quarterly payments per approved plan
- RP monitors compliance
- If default → Back to liquidation
```
